# Known gaps of the Lean library

This page is the register of every known gap between what the Lean library in this folder proves and what a user might take it to prove. It is kept for agents and auditors; the short list for people is in [THEOREM.md](THEOREM.md), "What it does not say". First come the [terms used on this page](#terms-used-on-this-page). Then section 1: the interface between the C++ programs and Lean · 2: the soundness theorem · 3: gaps at the theorem and its verifier · 4: theorems that are not about `theDescription`, with three entries (39, 10, 6) that are about `verify` and its system · 5: settled points, and numbers that are given under another entry · appendix: gap 15, weight hiding · 6: checks of which no run over these files is told here, the state of the files, and what the theorems of 39 do not reach · and an [index by number](#index-by-number).

**What is proved.** Soundness is proved in Lean 4 for the verifier written in Lean, for exact-match claims, in the random-oracle model for the main hash function, at public files fixed before that function is sampled, at the one full size; the C++ verifier (the default) and the CUDA prover are tested against it, not proved. The theorem and its conditions: 28. The tie to the programs: section 1.

Completeness and zero knowledge are proved in Lean for the protocol, at full size, each up to a small stated error: first for the interactive protocol (IOP), then for the compiled (non-interactive) prover and the same Lean verifier, in the random-oracle model. The honest prover in these theorems is a Lean definition, not the CUDA prover.

Completeness covers well-formed requests on which the model has a result and the claimed output is that result. Where the model has no result it says nothing. It is promised on `CanServe`, a condition on the weights and the request; what the honest prover refuses lies outside that promise. Zero knowledge holds against an honest verifier in the IOP and, after compiling, against any verifier within a bound on its hash queries and replies, on one copy of the table (a copy: a further encoding of the same weights under its own randomness, against which requests are proved); the simulator's running time is not modelled. Both are at the exact-match policy, with fresh random masks, for one commitment and one request per proof, not for batches. One bit per request is revealed (served or refused: a request is refused if it is ill-formed, if the model has no result on it, or if its result is other tokens), and it depends on the weights. A service that chooses to say "failed" reveals one label of three in its place (served, failed or other tokens). Neither completeness nor zero knowledge says anything of what the public files themselves tell of the weights, or of timing.

The theorems, each with its file and scope: 39. Checks of which no run over these files is told here, the state of the files, and what the theorems of 39 do not reach: section 6.

**Numbers and fields.** A gap is cited by its number alone: (14) is gap 14, "14 (f)" is its point (f), "15 (8)" is part (8) of the appendix, "section 1.1" is a section of this page; "LIMITS.md, L38" is a label on that page. A heading reads "### 14 · title"; two headings bear several numbers ("4, 9, 11" and "13, 3"). Numbers are fixed names: they are not in order, and some are not used. An entry has up to five fields. *The limit*: what is not proved, not tied or not tested (*The limit; proved in part*, where a part is proved). *Layer*: the protocol as written in Lean (its IOP, its statements and how its proofs are checked), the tie between Lean and the programs (a run of Lean code among them), or the programs. *Why it matters* to somebody who relies on an accept. *Proved* (or *Tested*) *nearest to it*, by Lean name and file. *Known attack or counterexample*.

**Tags.** What an entry calls proved, or a theorem, is a theorem of this library, checked by Lean's kernel; most are given with their Lean name and place. A place alone points at the text of a definition or a comment; a comment's word is no theorem.

Any other evidence is marked by a tag at the end of its sentence, or at the head of a field or list for all of it: [evaluated]: Lean code was run on stored values or files, by Lean's evaluator or by the command `lean-verify`, and no theorem states the outcome · [tested]: seen in a run of a program or script outside Lean (the C++ programs, the parser, the check scripts) · [read]: found by reading source text ([read in part]: only a part was read) · [counted]: found by a program that counts, searches or follows dependencies · [paper]: argued on paper. Words after a colon inside a tag give its reach ([read in part: seventeen files], [counted: once]). None of the five is a theorem or is machine-checked as a proof, and none has been independently reviewed.

**How to read an entry.** No attack on the protocol is known at any gap (for weight hiding: none that shows more than the design itself declares visible, docs/what-is-not-hidden.md, with the protocol's conditions kept: the appendix). "None known" means that none has been found, not that one was searched for: in the appendix only part (1) was attacked on purpose. Where a Lean statement is known to fail, or to be vacuous or met trivially, it is, at every gap listed, a fault of the statement, or of the form that stands for it, as typed; none is known to be the protocol's.

**Runs and counts.** Only two kinds of result are of this repository's own files: what Lean evaluates at every build (a `#guard` in a Lean file), and what this page or [CHECK-RESULTS.md](CHECK-RESULTS.md) tells in so many words as run over these files.

The 109 calls of the reference run that this page tells (the command `lean-verify`: 14, 76) and the calls on the 41 further proofs (14) were made over this repository's Lean files; the command and the parser were held by checksums before and after the calls [tested]; the 146 files of their tree outside lean/ that two traced calls beside them opened, files of the command and of the parser among them, have this repository's checksums (14, "Which programs (k)"; [CHECK-RESULTS.md](CHECK-RESULTS.md)) [tested: by a record that is not in this repository]. The call on the one proof more (14) was made in a folder whose compiled files had, before and behind an earlier call there, the checksum of this repository's build; no checksum was taken behind that call, and the folder's Lean files were not held [tested: by a record that is not in this repository].

One whole run of the full check over this repository's Lean files passed at every step; what it covers and what not: 18, 81 [tested]. One Lean file, with one definition and four theorems, which nothing else imports, was added after the runs reported here and checked on its own. Three of the check script's pinned values were changed and three of its stored lists were extended for it; nothing else in the script or the lists changed ([CHECK-RESULTS.md](CHECK-RESULTS.md)). The file is AtFullSide/Face/ExecutableVerifierIsLeanVerifier.lean (39). Each of the three lists (scripts/top-theorems.txt, scripts/claimed-theorems.tsv, scripts/front-statements.txt) has four entries more than at those runs. No run, build, count or pass reported on this page read that file, even where it is described as made over this repository's Lean files, over a build of them, over all the library's modules or over the script's lists, except four: the file's own check, the second checksum at 18, the count of the theorems that name `verdictOf` (37), and the count of 188 stored statements (section 6). The file's own check: Lean compiled that one module over a copy of a build of the other files, with no error and no warning; for the definition and for each of the four theorems Lean's output of axioms shows only Lean's three standard axioms; and Lean's kernel replayed the module twice without error, by the call that the check script makes for that folder and by `leanchecker`, each made apart from the script [tested]. No audit over all modules, no pass of the independent checker and no run of the full check that read the file is told here.

The counts told at 26 and 56 and the passes of the independent checker told at 18 (b), but for those on single theorems' exports that 18 (b) tells as made over this repository's files, were made over Lean files that differ from this repository's, in code too, and no repeat of any of these over this repository's files is told here (section 6).

The reference run's proof files, the two stored ones apart, are not in this repository, and 42 of them came labelled as made by a prover whose sources differ from this repository's (14).

Take any other run, evaluation, count, walk, scan, search, reading or pass of a proof checker that is told here (any that neither this page nor [CHECK-RESULTS.md](CHECK-RESULTS.md) tells as made over this repository's files) as made over files of the library and sources of the programs that differ from this repository's: it holds of this repository's code only as far as the files beneath it are the same in code as at that run, and of files that were not there it says nothing. Most cannot be repeated from this repository (45). Where a run is told, "honest", "lying", "true" and "false" are the labels that the files came with: nothing in the files shows that a label is right, and no run decided it (14).

**Names.** `verify` is the Lean verifier (Ladder.lean:416) at the protocol as Lean describes it, `theDescription`; `cp_verify` is the C++ verifier, `cp_commit` the C++ committing program and `cp_prove` the prover. Paths count from lean/ unless they begin with docs/, examples/, guide/, integrations/, tools/, blocks/ or paper/.

## Terms used on this page

They follow [TERMS.md](TERMS.md). Where the Lean files use another word, it is given in brackets.

Three parts of a proof are named in four ways:

| File | IOP | Honest prover | Soundness, round by round |
|---|---|---|---|
| key file | offline IOP | the key file's prover | `keySound` (2) |
| the copy's file | link IOP | the link proof's prover | `entrySound` (34) |
| proof file | online IOP | the request's prover | `proofSound` (1) |

Three verifiers appear: `verify`; its executable form ("the form"); and `cp_verify`, the C++ verifier. Beside them there is `verify-on-bytes`, a Lean text: this repository builds no stand-alone program from it (below). `innerCompiledVerifier` is the compiled verifier beneath `verify`. The command `lean-verify` (tools/lean-verify/lean_verify.sh) is a shell script that has the prover's raw files parsed and evaluates the executable form inside Lean; it is no compiled program of `verify` (45). "Compiled" has two senses: the compiler turns the interactive oracle proof into an argument by hashing, and `innerCompiledVerifier` is its compiled verifier; natively compiled code is machine code made from a Lean definition (46).

- **Index, instance, witness.** The index is the weights. An instance is a prompt with its output tokens. The relation has no witness.
- **Index verification key**: the 256-bit commitment ID alone (`Commitment`). The key file, the copy's file and the proof file come with the proof (10). **The extractor**: the index extractor.
- **Soundness**, or **index binding**: index-binding knowledge soundness, in its witness-free case (what is extracted: the weights alone): `Sound`; in general form `HasIndexBinding`. **Correctness of extraction**: `ExtractsCommittedWeights`. **Binding of the commitment, in the opening form**: `BindsWith`, the second clause of `SoundAndBinding`, the goals G9 and G9bits. **No two openings**: `NoTwoOpenings`. **Completeness**: `Complete`, `CompleteOn`, `ProofAcceptedWhere`, the goal G3'pol.
- **Index privacy** (of an argument system, not of an IOP): `HasIndexPrivacy`, which is weaker than the definition in spite of its name ([TERMS.md](TERMS.md)). `ZeroKnowledge` is a form of it with a stronger environment and a narrower key, and `ZeroKnowledgeOn` is that form for a smaller relation, on a named set of requests (20). **Hiding** is the wider word: it takes in hiding for batches and honest-verifier zero knowledge of an IOP, which are not index privacy. **Weight hiding** is the word of the pages outside lean/; Lean's form of it is zero knowledge, also called index privacy.
- **`verify`**, the Lean verifier: the term `verify` (Ladder.lean:416) at `theDescription`. `innerCompiledVerifier` is the compiled verifier that `verify` calls. Under OnBytes/ and Ask/, on pages and in comments, "verify()" stands for `innerCompiledVerifier`, taken at the two IOPs of Johnson/Flat/TwoSizes.lean and not at `theDescription`, and "the theorem on verify()" is the theorem on `innerCompiledVerifier`, `innerCompiledVerifier_sound` (13, 3), not the soundness theorem (28) (LIMITS.md, L53).

  **The soundness theorem**: `sound_of_verify_at` (28), the theorem on `verify`. Other pages of this folder write "the soundness theorem on `verify`".

  **The Lean verifier's system**: the argument system of the soundness theorem, with `verify` as its verifier and the IOP's own proof format, proof parser and honest side (28). **The constant honest side**: that honest side: the constant committer (a committer that returns one constant whatever the weights, and so commits to nothing; the committer of the six lines (39) is another: the one that `compile` makes (Johnson/Face/Compiler.lean), which encodes the weights and hashes the key) and a constant prover, which sends five empty files whatever the weights (55).

  **Fixed-message provers**, in the theorems on hiding, are another thing: provers that send the same messages whatever the weights, and so show nothing of them; at public files in use the protocol's honest provers are others: only where a function that makes them refuses do they fall back on these (75; the appendix).

  **Illustrative system**: a system that keeps the verifier and the extractor of the Lean verifier's system and has an illustrative committer: a committer written in Lean beside the IOP, not the compiled system's, that computes its commitment from the weights and its coins, so that the statements that read the honest committer have some content; no theorem ties it to a program (55, 64, 72; TERMS.md).

  **The honest part** (`honestProversAndSettings`, AtFullSide/Face/HonestProversAndSettingsValue.lean): the protocol's honest side as written in Lean, as definitions and not as programs. **The compiled system**: `compile (flashProve setup)`, where `flashProve setup` is `flashProveAtFiles` at the setup's public files, at their settings and at the honest part (AtFullSide/Face/TheSixLines.lean:82-84): another system with `verify` as its verifier (39).
- **The executable form**: a computable Lean function that a theorem ties to `verify` (56). It is evaluated inside Lean; it is no built program. **`verify-on-bytes`** (OnBytes/): the Lean text below `OnBytes.Main`: a hand translation of `cp_verify` into Lean, from which this repository builds no stand-alone program; one test (tools/accepts/) compiles it and has Lean load the compiled code while it evaluates files of lean/Ask/. Of its definitions `verify` uses some, through its scope test and the reading of the public files, and contains some as data that nothing applies (58); as a whole it is not `verify`.
- **IOP** (files: "description"): an interactive oracle proof as Lean writes it. The whole protocol is one IOP in three parts (39), and each part is an IOP too: the offline, the link and the online IOP (the table above); "the IOP", with no more said, is the whole. **The protocol as Lean describes it**, `theDescription`: the IOP from which `verify` is compiled; that it is the protocol that the programs run is not proved (section 1). **Sub-protocol** (files: "block"). **Kind**: the sub-protocols of the first chain fall into thirteen kinds, one piece of Lean text each. **Connecting rows**: the 77 book-keeping steps between them. **Law**: the soundness lemma of one sub-protocol, as Lean states it.
- **Chain**: one Fiat–Shamir hash chain. A proof file has three: a request's own ("the first chain"), the batch's shared proximity test ("the shared chain") and a last one; with the key file's three and the copy's file's one that makes seven. **Run value**: the value that salts a chain at its start (for most chains a hash over 32 random bytes).
- **Key file**: ivk/key.bin: the root of the first committed table, with its OOD answers, and the committer's one-time proof that the table is well formed. Its folder's name notwithstanding, in Lean's statements it is no part of the index verification key: it is sent with the proof (10). **Copy**: a further encoding of the same weights under its own randomness; requests are proved against it. **The copy's file** (files: "entry file", "leaf file"): its root, its OOD answers and its link proof. **Link proof**, **link IOP**: a proof meant to show that two committed tables encode the same weights; the relation for which the link IOP is proved sound has three arms: the first word does not decode, or the further word has a collision, or both decode to the same index (34).
- **OOD sample** (out-of-domain sample) of a committed table: its OOD seed and its OOD answers (files: "the record" for the answers; the type `Iop.HolographicProtocol.Rec` contains the pair). The OOD answers are masked sums of the weights. **Query bound** of a committed table: how many of its columns all proofs together may open before its encoding randomness stops covering them. **Commitment ID**: 32 bytes: a hash of a digest of the public files' bytes, of the first committed table's root and shape, and of its OOD sample. In Lean's statements these 32 bytes are "the commitment".
- **The public files**: the parameter file, the layout file and the scale file. **The plan**: the list of rounds with each round's error bound, computed from the parameter file. **In scope**: 35. **The two stored sets** of public files: those of the two stored full-size examples, examples/full-size/ (exact match) and examples/full-size-clipped-gap/ (the policy (25856, 512, 1)). **The written copy** of a stored set (`VerifierAndGoals.storedPublicFiles`, `VerifierAndGoals.zGap`): the library's own copy of these files, typed as a Lean term. No theorem ties it to the files on disk; a script, scripts/check_stored_examples.py, compares the two (73 (k)). **The side library** (AtFullSide/): a library outside the main library, built with a larger thread stack for one constant (52, 73).
- **The gate**: those tests on the public files that `verify` gathers in `protocolDataWellFormed`, before its three decisions (on the key file, the copy's file and the proof file). It is not all that `verify` tests on these files: the test of the scope (`VerifierAndGoals.InScope`) is in front of it, two reads of the files (readConfig, readMore) are outside it, and the outer branches of the decision on the copy's file are beside it; further such places are named, and no theorem says that the list is complete (73). **Entry tests**: the fourteen tests of Johnson/Desc/Laid/ProofAsks.lean, a part of the gate.
- **Acceptance policy**, or policy: three numbers in the parameter file: the clip C, the bound theta on the mean cost, the limit cap. A token's cost is 256 plus its gap: for a token of the vocabulary, how far its logit lies below the model's first choice's, plus 1 if the first choice has the smaller token number, in the unit of the given weights' own logits (Policy/Front/MeetsPolicy.lean:53-65; `logitGap_eq_choice`, Policy/Front/MeetsPolicyGuards.lean:109-117). An output meets the policy if (a) the counted tokens' costs, each clipped at C, sum to at most theta times their number, (b) at most cap counted tokens cost more than C, and (c) a token that follows a full window is the model's next token (Policy/Front/MeetsPolicy.lean:111-164). **Exact match**: the policy (256, 256, 0): the output is not empty and every output token is the model's next token. **Tolerant policy**: any other.
- **True claim, false claim**: a claim is a commitment, a prompt and an output. It is true if the output meets the policy of the run's own parameter file at the committed weights: at exact match, if it is the model's greedy next tokens; under a tolerant policy a true claim's output may differ from the greedy tokens, as the second stored set's stored reply does at one place (14). That is the runs' sense; the theorems speak of the weights that the extractor reads from the commitment (28).
- **The parser**: tools/parser/, a program outside Lean. **A proof parser**, in a goal or a theorem, is another thing: a deterministic Lean function inside the statement, with access to the hash function, from the prompt, the output and a proof in any format to `verify`'s typed proof; it does not see the commitment and cannot reject (Ladder.lean:394-398). **The IOP's own proof parser** reads the five byte strings that the parser writes. **Raw files, parsed form**: the raw files are what the prover writes; the parser turns them into five byte strings, the parsed form, which Lean reads. `verify` reads no raw file (section 1).
- **Full size, small size**: two parameter sets that the library writes out as Lean terms: that of the stored full-size example, and that of a smaller stored example (61). **Can be served** (`CanServe`, VerifiableInference/CanServe.lean): side conditions on a prompt and output tokens, at given settings and weights: every token is in the vocabulary, the output is not empty, and the model has a value on the whole window. **Typed**: stated as a Lean term of a stated type, as against bytes of a file. A typed proof is what `innerCompiledVerifier` takes from the prover's side (`TypedProof`, Ladder.lean). A typed query is a query to one of several independent random oracles, over which the compiler is stated; one typed query can be many strings of the one hash function. **Session**: one call of the prover.
- **The second hash function**: the format's second, non-standard hash function, fixed, built on BLAKE2b's compression function (60, 7).
- **The goals**: the statements that this library asks of an IOP. `scripts/check.py` pins twenty-nine Lean files: some state the goals or what the goals are written in (the goals also name definitions beneath the twenty-nine, which are not pinned: LIMITS.md, L76), some state forms in which no goal is written, and the others contain theorems and tests about them (50).

  **The six top statements** (VerifiableInference/Statements.lean): `Sound`, `SoundAndBinding` (soundness with binding of the commitment, in the opening form), `SoundOpened` (soundness at weights that the adversary opens itself), `Complete`, `ZeroKnowledge`, and completeness, soundness and zero knowledge together.

  **Nearest form**: for the three statements on soundness, the top statement itself, asked at each set of public files in scope whose policy is exact match; for `Complete` no nearest form is stated as a goal but one under a policy (G3'pol: 39, "The six top statements as written", (e); 52); for `ZeroKnowledge`, and for the three together, no nearest form is stated as a goal (39, "The six top statements as written", (d), (f)).

  **The six lines**: six theorems about `compile` at `flashProve` (AtFullSide/Face/TheSixLines.lean): `sound`, `soundAndBinding`, `completeOnCanServe`, `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe`, `refusedHandsOutNoProof`, `repliesFit`; beside them the definition `leanVerifier` (39). These six lines are the library's goals on the compiled prover and verifier; elsewhere on this page "goal" means a proposition that Ladder.lean, Ladder/Bits.lean or Ladder/Package.lean states about any IOP (`VerifierAndGoals.Description`).
- **MCA**: mutual correlated agreement. **Escape form**: 49. **Vacuous**: true because its hypothesis is never met, so that it says nothing. **Trivially**, of zero knowledge or index privacy: met by a prover that shows nothing. **Closed** test or condition: one on public data alone, with nothing left free.

  **The definition**: the definition that this library follows (ZKCP/Defs.lean; its source: CREDITS.md). **The paper**: paper/paper.pdf. **The technical report**: a technical report on this protocol that is not public and is not in this repository; what is taken from it or compared with it has the tag [paper] (16).

  **The independent checker**: nanoda (18). **Walk**: at a Merkle tree (14, 66), the verifier's way from the opened leaves up toward the root; everywhere else, a program that follows dependencies, by import lines or, inside Lean, from constant to constant; it shows which declarations occur, not what each is used for, and proves nothing.

  **The reference run**: the run of `lean-verify` of which this page gives the figures of 109 calls (14, 76); over which files: "Runs and counts". **The full check**: the library's whole check, scripts/check.py; where this page gives its outcome in 18's table: one whole run over this repository's Lean files; other outcomes, over other files: 18 (h), 73 (h).
- **Public files in use**: in scope, accepted by the gate, read without error, and at exact match (`FilesInUse`, Johnson/Face/FinalStatement.lean; 39).

## 1. The interface between the C++ programs and Lean

The Lean library is about Lean code. The C++ programs enter in one way: a Lean verifier has to accept the proofs that the C++ prover makes. Three verifiers appear on this page: `verify`, with `innerCompiledVerifier` beneath it; its executable form; and `cp_verify`, the C++ verifier.

- **Proved.** The soundness of `verify` (28) trusts neither the parser nor any C++ program: what a parser computes counts as sent by the prover (Ladder.lean:496-497), so the soundness theorem takes the five byte strings of a proof from the adversary. A wrong parsed file can cause an honest proof to be rejected, and can do no more than a prover can [read]. The executable form is tied to `verify` by a theorem at all public files and on every parsed proof file, whenever the hash function's answers have 32 bytes (56).
- **Not proved.** Nothing is proved of a C++ program, the verifier that users run by default among them, nor that these programs run the protocol that `verify` checks. A parser outside Lean (tools/parser/), unproved, turns the prover's raw files into the five byte strings. `verify` reads no raw file of the prover. That step is outside Lean: no hypothesis speaks of it, and no theorem ties the parser's output to the raw bytes. No compiled program runs `verify`: the command `lean-verify` evaluates the executable form inside Lean (45, 56).
- **What there is instead.** Runs, at full size and on synthetic weights, of the executable form, evaluated inside Lean by `#eval` with natively compiled parts loaded [evaluated]. Their records are at (14), (76) and (66); THEOREM.md, "What has been run", gives the short telling. In the reference run all 47 honest proofs were accepted (14). None of the 57 lying proofs was accepted: 54 were rejected, 24 of them made for a false reply and 30 claiming the true reply with a lie inside the proof, and at three the command's parser refused the file before Lean was called, which is no verdict and no rejection (76). The other five of the run's 109 calls were negative tests of a shallow kind, an honest proof beside a claim that is not its own; all five were rejected (76). In other runs some altered parsed proof files of true claims were accepted, with no false claim among them (14). In one run, of six placed changes of one bit of the parsed proof file, four were accepted, the claim being true, and at three of these `verify` and `cp_verify` disagree (66).
- **Known attack.** None known on the protocol at any gap of this section; the altered files above are of true claims (66).

### 1.1 What crosses, value by value

The same values cross for the small-size checks (folder Ask/) and for `verify`; what Lean does with them is not the same. The third column is what the files of the small-size checks say. Each cell gives one state: recomputed, compared, tested, taken as given, not tested or not known, whole or in part. The fourth column contains what is known; it is not complete. The details are at (17), (37), (41) and (66); the terms are at the head of the page.

| Value | Who makes it | The small-size checks under Ask/ | `verify` | Where said |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Commitment ID, request, reply, the three public files | the user | taken as given, from the user, never from the parser | taken as given, from the user (37) | Ladder.lean:400-412; Ask/README.md |
| The public files' digest | Lean, by one hash query | recomputed (`readConfig`) | recomputed | Ladder.lean:322-326 |
| The plan; the four lookup tables | Lean, from the public files; its planner follows the C++ programs' planner | recomputed; no table is compared with the file (17) | recomputed from the public files' bytes | Ask/README.md; Johnson/G/RoundsOfPlan.lean:116-125; LIMITS.md, V3 |
| The division of a key file or proof file into rounds and openings | the parser, which must replay the hash chains to find the boundaries | recomputed: the hash chain over the messages | the same parser; what `verify` recomputes of it is not known | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:296-303, 323-327 |
| The run values of the first and third chains and of the link's chain | **the parser** (a hash over 32 random bytes that the parsed file drops) | **taken as given** | taken as given | Ask/README.md; Ask/AllCompiledSmall.lean:80-81, 120-121 |
| The run value of the batch's shared proximity test | the parser | recomputed and compared | **taken as given** | Ask/ProofCompiledSmall.lean:44-45 |
| Whole Merkle paths | the parser, from the file's joint openings | compared: followed to the roots; unread entries are free | compared in the same way [read]; altered entries were accepted (14, 66) | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:299-300, 345; LIMITS.md, V0 |
| The root of the Merkle tree over the batch's requests; the request's leaf index, the number of leaves, the leaf's salt and path | the parser: the root computed, the rest copied | compared in part (17) | tested in part (17) | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:330-334, 383-386; OnBytes/Maker/Page/GuardVerify.lean:6-11 |
| Which leaves are opened | the parser: computed (the positions are sampled from the hash) | not tested (17) | not known | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:297-298, 349-350 |
| The committed table's root | the parser: copied; it is the parser's claim (41) | compared with the commitment ID | this field of the parsed proof file is tested for its length alone; the table is opened under the copy's file's root, and the key file's root is read [read] (66 (f)) | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:379-382; Ask/AllCompiledSmall.lean:48-53 |
| The committed table's OOD answers | the parser: copied; they are the parser's claim (41) | compared with the commitment ID | `verify` reads them [read] (66 (f)); what it holds them against is not known | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:379-382; Ask/AllCompiledSmall.lean:48-53 |
| The copy's number | the parser: copied; it is the parser's claim (41) | taken as given: nothing holds it (41) | tested against the proof's own front round alone (41) | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:379-382; Ask/AllCompiledSmall.lean:65-66 |
| The OOD seeds | a side file of the parser's, in none of the prover's files | recomputed by each check; the side file is for comparison alone | not known (17) | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:12-13, 352-354 |
| The next token | the prover's message in the first chain | compared with the user's reply (37) | compared with the user's reply; a false reply was rejected in runs at full size (14 (i), 76) | OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:369-374 |
| Words of field elements below the prime | the parser: copied | tested by the decoder and the typed decision, but not for the OOD answers | tested inside `verify`'s own text | LIMITS.md, V10 |
| The copy's file | read twice: by the parser, and raw by a converter written in Lean | compared, through the proof file's table alone | not known | Ask/AllCompiledSmall.lean:67-68; Ask/EntryRecord.lean:37-39 |

The parser's second script rebuilds the original file byte for byte at every run, at the full size too; it needs a side file that Lean never reads [tested].

### 1.2 What has been run on the programs' files

| Verifier | Reads | Has been run on | Outcome | What that is worth |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `verify`, through its executable form | flat binary data that the parser writes, read by short Lean functions that hash nothing; the user's inputs from the user's side | full-size proofs in their parsed form and altered copies of them; synthetic weights (the command of tools/lean-verify/ takes the raw files and has them parsed itself) | honest proofs (14); lying proofs (76); altered files (14, 66); forms with fewer tests (14 (i)) | [evaluated]; tied to `verify` by a theorem (56) |
| `innerCompiledVerifier` at the two IOPs of Johnson/Flat/TwoSizes.lean, and parts of it under Ask/ | typed values; parsed files of the **small** example (examples/accepted-proof/; there is a second small stored example, examples/accepted-proof-clipped-gap/); raw files of the small and the full-size example for the copy's file's closed check and one chain of the key file | the small example's parsed files, in three checks (the key file; the proof file; the three files joined), with 6, 6 and 5 negative tests; those raw files | for the copy's file an accept is told, at the small example, beside a flipped bit in the key file that is accepted too and refusals, on files, that were the file reader's own, not the closed check's (Ask/EntryFilesSmall.lean:20-27, :46-51); for the six runs on parsed files (each of the three checks, without and with its negative tests) the outcome is not recorded under lean/ | It gives no soundness: it is a test. At the two IOPs (`innerCompilerInputFromDryRunAtFullSize`, `innerCompilerInputFromDryRunAtSmallSize`) it has no compiled code (LIMITS.md, V6). `verify` rejects the small example's files, so no run at the small size can count (Ladder.lean:203-207, 414-415) |

An illustrative committer has been compared with `cp_commit` in values alone, at one small example: 55, row `theCpp`.

### 1.3 How far Lean is independent of the C++ formats

An interface independent of the C++ formats would have four properties: the C++ programs' file formats stay unknown to Lean; an untrusted converter outside Lean turns a proof's content into plain data; every string that goes into the hash is built by Lean encoders whose laws are proved; and the two sides are compared one hash call at a time. The table has nine points, and says of each whether it holds.

| Point | State at `verify` |
|---|---|
| Lean reads no C++ format | **Holds in part.** No key file or proof file is read; the three public files' bytes are (`readFiles`, `InScope`) |
| The user's inputs reach `verify` directly | **Holds by type** (Ladder.lean:400-412). What turns the user's files into these arguments is outside `verify` (37). The padding tokens after the checked range are sent with the proof |
| An untrusted converter converts | **Holds in part.** The goal on soundness ranges over every proof parser within a query bound, and so does the theorem of section 2.2. The converting part exists (the parser); it takes its counts and lengths from `cp_verify`'s configuration code, compiled in (21). The goals' `verify` (its top four lines) and four fields of `Description` are typed otherwise than this point asks (Ladder.lean:382-385, 404-412), in a file that states the goals (50) |
| The proof is plain data; no test in front of `verify` | **Does not hold** (17). `TypedProof` depends on the public files and on the honest provers' types (Ladder.lean:339-354); in the protocol as Lean describes it `TypedProof` is plain at the top level, not further down |
| A short function in Lean parses the plain data that the converter hands over | **Holds.** The format is specified nowhere among the format files under docs/format/ (21) |
| The three public files are what an encoder gives | **Does not hold.** `verify` takes the three files' bytes and parses them inside Lean |
| One coding of every typed hash query, with its laws | **Holds in part** (22). One construction gives an instance for each value of eight public digests, each one to one on all typed queries, its laws proved; three groups of calls go round it |
| From digest to challenge: the map named, its distance from uniform counted | **Holds in part, for one file.** For the copy's file the distance enters the error of the rounds that were looked at. One factor in an OOD seed has two distributions: uniform non-zero in Lean; in the format the first non-zero of at most two sampled elements, else 1 (they differ by about one over the field's size squared) |
| A test, call by call, against a log of `cp_verify`'s hash calls | **Does not hold.** The two logs are not equal call for call. Every call of `cp_verify` for the rounds' challenges occurs in the form's log, at one half at least, with the same answer; the form asks 142,924 more: as far as the logs show, the tails of rounds of which `cp_verify` computes a prefix (22) |

### 1.4 The gaps at the interface

Its entries: 14 · 17 · 21 · 22 · 23 · 25 · 37 · 57 · 59 · 66 · 67 · 68 · 4, 9, 11 · 76.

### 14 · That `verify` accepts the C++ prover's proofs is tested, not proved

- **The limit.** No theorem says that `verify` accepts the C++ prover's proofs. Completeness is proved of the honest provers written in Lean (39); no theorem ties them to the C++ prover. What there is: runs of the executable form, recorded under "Tested nearest to it". These are their limits. (See also 52 and 55.)
  - (a) Every run is an evaluation. What a run trusts: (45).
  - (b) "True", "false", "honest", "lying" and "lies inside the proof" are the labels that the files came with. No run decided them, and nothing in the files shows which program made the files.
  - (c) The proofs are at two sets of public files, which differ in the parameter file alone: the first stored set (examples/full-size/, exact match) and the second (examples/full-size-clipped-gap/, the tolerant policy of 44). They are on synthetic weights and have little variety. Each of the 47 accepted proof files contains one request and is at one of the 64 places of a batch's tree, 46 of them at another place than the first, with a path of six digests [counted: by a record that is not in this repository]; how many requests the batch had is not in a proof file. By the records of the runs that made the 42 files that came labelled as the prover's, each of these was made either in a call of one request or in a service's batch of 24 requests [tested: by a record that is not in this repository]; which of the 42 came which way has not been looked up, and the files were not opened for it.
  - (d) Honest proofs had been accepted by the C++ verifier before they were put to `verify`: the reference run's files `cp_verify` had judged before, in runs of its own [tested]; in the other runs, by the label that the files came with. So another verifier's verdict on each was known before `verify` was called. The reference run was not blind: all of these 109 calls had been made before on the same files, and the outcomes expected of it, fixed before the first of the run's 109 calls, were the outcomes of those earlier calls; `cp_verify` had judged every set too (how sets and calls were paired: "Which programs (k)").
  - (e) The lying proofs are few in kind, and all that were made for a false reply were rejected at one place inside one step [evaluated] (76).
  - (f) Under a tolerant policy an accept says less than at exact match, and no run shows the policy's own test rejecting anything.
  - (g) Nine altered parsed proof files of true claims were accepted. No false claim is among them.
  - (h) That an accepted proof cannot be altered into another accepted proof is not proved of any verifier (66).
  - (i) Some results are of forms of the verifier with fewer entry tests than `verify` has.
  - (j) No theorem ties the written copy to the public files on disk, and the library does not test that those files have the written copy's checksums (Ladder/Stored.lean:17-18). A shipped script compares them: 73 (k).
  - (k) The two stored full-size proofs, the only full-size ones in this repository, were not made by this repository's prover.
  - (l) Only the runs on the stored full-size proofs can be repeated from this repository (45).
- **Layer.** The tie between Lean and the programs.
- **Why it matters.** A gap here could only cause a visible rejection (52). The accepted altered files leave soundness as it is (66). An accept of a stored proof is not an accept of a proof that the reader's own build of the prover made.
- **Proved nearest to it.** Where the hash function's answers have 32 bytes, the form's verdict is `verify`'s decision (`PV.Ride.theForm_eq_verify`, Johnson/PVR/SoundOfTheForm.lean:87-91; 56). So, as far as Lean's evaluator, compiler and runtime are trusted, each accept was that of `verify` as it stood at that call, at the real hash function, and as far as these runs go that `verify` did not reject everything. The reference run and the calls on the 41 further proofs (told below) were made over this repository's Lean files ("Runs and counts"); the call on the one proof more was made in a folder whose compiled files had this repository's build's checksum before and behind an earlier call there, and whose Lean files were not held ("Runs and counts"); for no other run is the tie to this repository's `verify` held (26). That `verify` accepts the proofs of the honest part written in Lean is proved in the random-oracle model (39); at BLAKE2b no theorem shows an acceptance (52).
- **Tested nearest to it.** Each verdict of the form or of `lean-verify` below is [evaluated], and each of `cp_verify` or the parser [tested]. `verify` was always given the parsed form of the prover's files.
  - *Honest proofs ((a), (c), (d)).* In the reference run, at full size and on synthetic weights, the executable form, natively compiled in part, accepted all 47 honest proofs that were put to it: 45 at exact match and 2 under the second stored set's tolerant policy. `cp_verify` accepted the same 47 files, in runs of its own (from which sources: (k)) [tested]. By the label that they came with, 42 of the 47 are the prover's (41 at exact match, one under the tolerant policy); the other five are the two stored proofs and three honest proofs of test programs. Over this repository's Lean files the command has also been called on 41 further proofs, none among the 47, all at exact match and on synthetic weights, which came labelled as proofs of the timed runs; it accepted each ([CHECK-RESULTS.md](CHECK-RESULTS.md)) [evaluated]. One proof more, of one request, at exact match and on synthetic weights, under a commitment of its own, was made by builds of `cp_commit` and `cp_prove` that their builders report as of this repository's sources; `cp_verify` had accepted it beforehand; the command, over this repository's Lean files as far as "Runs and counts" tells, accepted it [evaluated].

    The proofs have little variety: 47 proofs of 47 distinct claims, most of them of one next token, of 39 requests of 2 shapes, at 7 commitments [counted]. That the commitments of the prover's three runs (told two paragraphs below: "of 723 that it made in three runs") are to one set of synthetic weights is what their makers say; of the other four it is known only that the weights are synthetic. These 42 are a part, taken by a rule fixed beforehand, of 364 proofs by that prover (363 at exact match, one under the stored tolerant policy) that the command had accepted, every one, in other calls, over Lean files that differ from this repository's, in code too; none is counted twice here. Of the 364 only the 42 are in the reference run.

    The compiled Lean files of those other calls had been built beforehand; that they were built from those Lean sources is not shown by the files. These calls fall under the head's rule for other runs, and the code of the command's parser's compiled C++ program was looked at in none of them.

    Of the 364, the 363 at exact match are of 723 that it made in three runs, 241 a run, by its makers' word: of each run the one proof made outside its two services and the 120 of the first service, by a rule fixed before the runs. The 363 have little variety: 360 of them are proofs of one next token, of 96 requests of one shape, from one list, each with one reply, at three commitments: 288 distinct claims, 216 of them proved once and 72 twice; all 363 were judged at the first stored set's three public files [counted]. The one under the tolerant policy is at a commitment of its own.

    The rule for the 42, fixed before any call of the reference run: run by run, in the list's order, a proof is taken if no proof of its request was taken before it, twelve a run; the three made outside a service; the one under the tolerant policy; and the two honest proofs behind two of the negative tests (76). Nothing of a proof but its request's checksum was looked at; that no proof was picked by its content is not shown by the files. The other 322 were not put to `verify` in the reference run. The other 360 of the 723, the 120 of each run's second service, were not kept (by report), and were never put to `verify`.
  - *Altered inputs (g).* Verdicts of the form on 41 inputs, each altered in one place:

    | Input altered | Inputs | Rejected | Accepted |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | the claimed reply | 5 | 5 | 0 |
    | a public file | 5 | 5 | 0 |
    | the parsed key file | 8 | 8 | 0 |
    | the parsed copy's file | 6 | 6 | 0 |
    | the parsed proof file | 17 | 8 | 9 |
    | All | 41 | 32 | 9 |

    Each of the nine is an alteration of the parsed proof file, made after the parser had run, eight at exact match and one under the tolerant stored policy, with bits flipped in Merkle-path entries that the verifier's walk does not take [read]; runs agree with that reading. The commitment, prompt, output and public files were the honest ones, so no claim false by the run's own policy was accepted. Two of the nine had been expected to be rejected.
    - Six of the nine came from 26 altered inputs of six runs, 20 of which were rejected, by a count made by hand from the runs' reports: the two at the middle byte (the next point), and the four of (66), placed by program at entries that `verify` does not take. Three of those four lie at digests that the prover sent, and there `verify` and `cp_verify` disagree (66). Three more were accepted in two further runs: one of eight altered inputs at one honest proof, and two of seven at the first stored full-size proof (examples/full-size/).
    - At each of two honest proofs, one at each stored set, neither of them a stored proof, of five altered inputs four were rejected and one accepted: the parsed proof file with one bit of its middle byte flipped. At the two stored proofs the same change fell in a digest that the proof file contains, which is read and hashed toward the root, and it was rejected. So the middle byte is no negative test: whether a change there must be rejected depends on where the middle falls.
    - Why such a file is accepted, and what is proved of the free entries: (66). Not tried: a request with padding, another copy, real weights.
  - *Under a tolerant policy (f).* At the second stored set (the policy: 44) the stored proof's output departs from the greedy tokens at one place; that rests on one run of the integer-model package (`integrations/vllm/integer_model/`, in Python, outside Lean), on sources of the package that differ from this repository's, not on the proof; whether that package and Lean's integer model agree at full size has not been compared. That set's honest proofs were accepted, among them one at a new commitment, of that example's own request, which `cp_verify` found within the policy's limits and rejected without the policy (that it was the call without the policy that printed the rejection rests on a report alone).

    At the stored proof, all five altered inputs were rejected and none reached the policy's own test: a changed output and a changed proof file failed the check of the proof file, a changed key file and a changed copy's file failed the check of that file (the three files changed in their parsed form), and a changed layout file put the public files out of scope. So this shows an accept under a tolerant policy, not that the policy's test rejects anything. Under that policy `lean-verify` has been put to no lying proof, of a false claim or of a true one, and to no negative test. Files beyond the policy's limits: (76).
  - *Forms with fewer entry tests (i).* Three runs at full size were made at the stored full-size proof. Two of them were of forms of the verifier with eight and thirteen entry tests, not fourteen. On those forms 34 different alterations (33 of an input, one of a hash answer) were all rejected: 15 in front of the three files' steps (three by the scope test, three by the reader of the parsed files in front of the verifier, seven at the commitment ID's test, two where the request is built) and 19 at the three files' steps. At a false reply the proof file's decision was reached and rejected, and nothing else did. One bit of the middle byte of the copy's file was rejected on every road (`cp_verify`; the parser; the form), always in front of that file's decision. A well-formed false copy's file reached that file's decision and was rejected by it (33). Both outcomes rest on a report alone.

    At every placed change of one bit of a parsed file in these three runs the compiled verifier's own checks reject before the file's decision is reached, so these one-bit tests do not reach the decisions on the files. The six placed changes of (66) are another run.
  - *The fourteen entry tests.* A file of `#eval` commands asked them in turn. At the stored files all fourteen accept, and at each of five altered values the fourteenth rejects, at three of them it alone [evaluated]; the alterations are of a Lean value, not of a file. In none of those three runs does one of the entry tests 9 to 13 reject. That they accepted is no printed line: it follows, by a theorem, from the printed line which says that the entry tests accepted. The gate, of which they are a part, passes at the written copy (73).
    - No build from scratch and no kernel re-check stood beneath that evaluation. The entry tests ran interpreted, other parts as natively compiled code. Its driver was not the form's file, Johnson/PVR/RideThe.lean, and no theorem says that the two are equal (26). Two further trials called that file itself, with thirteen entry tests, not fourteen: the honest proof was accepted and one bit of the proof file rejected.
  - *Which programs (k).* But for the Lean files of the reference run and of the calls on the 41 further proofs, which are this repository's ("Runs and counts"; of the call on the one proof more: the same paragraph), and for what is told below of their command and parser, the results told here with their own figures were made over programs (`cp_verify`: the next sentence) and Lean files that differ from this repository's. Two builds of `cp_verify` judged the sets of files of the reference run's 109 calls, each in runs of its own, with the same outcomes: the 47 honest sets accepted, the other 62 (the 57 lying ones and the five negative tests) rejected; one build has the checksum of a `cp_verify` built from this repository's sources (its runs were paired with the calls afterwards, by the label, the policy and the first 16 hex digits of the proof file's checksum; the other files of a set were not paired); the other build has another checksum, and from which sources it was built is not shown [tested: by a record that is not in this repository]. One difference in the programs' sources is known: in this repository's sources the class that feeds and finishes BLAKE2b, in one header that the three programs and the command's parser compile, is another text than in the programs of the altered-input results.

    The reference run repeats none of the altered-input results: by their labels no file that was put to it had been altered after it was made, and its five negative tests are honest proofs beside a claim that is not theirs (76). The program `cp_verify` was not called in these 109 calls of the reference run: so says the text of the job that made them [read]; the traces of two calls beside them, a small one, which ended before it reached the claim, and one on the stored full-size proof, which accepted, show no start of it [tested: by a record that is not in this repository]; none of the 109, and none of the calls on the 41 further proofs, was traced.

    The command compiles its parser's C++ program anew at every call, from the tree in which it stands. The compiled code had the same checksum at each of the 109 calls that reached it and at a control compiled apart; in these calls no compile with the other class was set beside it, so the checksum alone does not show which class was compiled. The calls that reached Lean loaded compiled Lean files made beforehand; the library's own compiled files, the command and the parser were held by checksums before and after the calls (the packages' compiled files were not). The 146 files of that tree outside lean/ that the two traced calls told above opened, among them files of the command and of the parser and the configuration files of the stored full-size example, have this repository's checksums, and all files that the compiler lists as parts of the parser's unit are among them [tested: by a record that is not in this repository]; the header with the class named above, which the parser compiles, is therefore one of them. That the compiled files were made from this repository's Lean files is Lake's finding, not the run's, at the build, apart from the full check's own, that [CHECK-RESULTS.md](CHECK-RESULTS.md) tells; that the command, the parser and the compiler that builds the parser read that tree's files and no others is not shown for these calls; what the traced calls opened outside the tree is not told here. The natively compiled part was not made by that build: the command compiled it itself, in a call of its own before these calls (but for three objects of a package, which stood in the tree before that call); its objects were held by checksums before and after the calls too. By the command's text it links them into one library anew at every call that passes the parser [read]; that library's checksum was printed only at the command's compiling call told above, and at none of these calls [tested: by a record that is not in this repository].

    The proofs were not made in the run. Those that came labelled as the prover's were made by programs whose sources differ from this repository's (docs/limits.md, "What was run on these programs", the row on `lean-verify`). All calls ran on one machine, beside other jobs. By the command's text each part is compiled by one compiler [read]; no compile line of the run was read, and the compilers, Python and Lean's installer were held by no checksum. No run has been made with a build that a reader made.
    - Seven proofs of the runs other than the reference run, all at full size and on synthetic weights, four at exact match and three under a tolerant policy, were accepted by `cp_verify` as well; for three of the seven there is only the label that the files came with. The two stored full-size proofs are among them. The first (examples/full-size/) is tied to the program said to have made it by the proof's size, not by a checksum. For the second (examples/full-size-clipped-gap/) there is that example's own page, and no record of the run is in this repository. One of the seven came from a cheating program set to alter nothing; its file is byte for byte the proof that an honest test program made for the same commitment and request [counted]. Another came from a test program's honest code.
  - *What can be repeated (l).* `tools/lean-verify/lean_verify.sh` runs on the stored proofs (45). It has accepted both, and has rejected the stored full-size proof under another commitment ID and under another last token (all four calls in the reference run). In other calls on the first stored proof the command was ended on purpose while it ran, by a signal or a time limit: these gave no verdict, and each time a call on the same files that ran to its end accepted the proof [evaluated]. All the other runs went on proof files that are not in this repository, some of them made by a test prover.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known: no false claim is known to pass, and no claim false by a run's own policy was accepted. The nine of (g) are altered files of true claims.

### 17 · `verify` does not recompute all that the parser computes, and some tests stand only in front of it

- **The limit.** What the parser computes and `verify` does not recompute counts as sent by the prover (section 1.1: the chains' run values are taken as given). Some thirty tests on the proof's values (lengths, counts, a run value that is not zero) stand only in the reader in front of the compiled verifier, or nowhere. For completeness the parser's values are taken as given.
  - (a) Plan: at the small-size checks both hash functions are called by name, and no table is compared with the file.
  - (b) Salts and leaves. At the small-size checks the number of leaves is compared with the plan, and a guard outside the compiled verifier alone tests the salt's length (OnBytes/Maker/Page/GuardVerify.lean:6-11). The reader does not test that the index is below the number of leaves. At `verify` the lengths of a chain's salt and of each opening's are tested inside the compiled verifier; the length of the salt of the request's leaf is tested nowhere.
  - (c) Opened leaves. At the small-size checks the reader does not test that the rows are just the leaves read.
  - (d) OOD seeds. One factor in an OOD seed has a type that says it is not zero, so whoever builds the value must test that.
- **Layer.** The tie between Lean and the programs.
- **Why it matters.** The parser is a matter of completeness and use. For soundness these tests count for nothing and are not needed (section 1), and the wider form of the soundness theorem holds for every proof format and every proof parser within n queries (section 2.2). That is the prover's side only: the public files, the commitment ID, the prompt and the output must reach `verify` from the user (37).
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known. No wrong accept follows from the untested length of the salt of the request's leaf [read].

### 21 · The parser depends on `cp_verify`'s code, and its output format is specified nowhere

- **The limit.** The parser takes its counts and lengths from `cp_verify`'s configuration code, compiled in (tools/parser/parse_files.sh:126-130), and replays the hash chains, because the prover's files contain no lengths. Its output format is no part of the format that docs/format/ specifies. One raw file has many parsed files and several raw files have one (LIMITS.md, V0).
- **Layer.** The tie between Lean and the programs.
- **Why it matters.** A verifier behind the parser and `cp_verify` need not agree on every file.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known for soundness. No raw file on which the two disagree has been made: the disagreement of (66) is at parsed files that the parser did not write.

### 22 · What is hashed: one coding, not equal to `cp_verify`'s log call for call

- **The limit.** That `verify` hashes exactly what the C++ prover hashed is a theorem only at queries that pass the coding's own tests. That the verifier asks such queries alone is stated of no verifier (Johnson/Top/ProofPage.lean:56), so it rests on the accepting runs at full size.
  - The coding is one construction, not one instance: for each value of eight public digests, which `verify` itself gets from the hash, it gives an instance that is one to one on all typed queries, its laws proved (a query outside the honest shape gets a string of its own after an escape byte: 49).
  - Three groups of calls go round the coding: the readings of the parameters, the commitment ID, the second hash function.
  - The two logs are not equal call for call ((a) and (b) below). No table lists all hash calls.
- **Layer.** The tie between Lean and the programs.
- **Why it matters.** The comparison changes no verdict. Its (a) is a poor fit and a cost, and says nothing on soundness. Of (b) the cause is seen in the logs and by reading, and is no theorem; the soundness theorem is about `verify` as its text stands (28).
- **Tested nearest to it.** One comparison [counted], on the stored full-size proof (examples/full-size/), over Lean files that differ from this repository's, of the executable form (accepting), evaluated at a hash that writes down each query, against the log of a test build of this repository's `cp_verify` (docs/limits.md, "What was run on these programs"). That build is not in this repository; it is tied to the released program by its count of compressions and by its printed output, not by its bytes.

  A call of `cp_verify` for a round's challenges gives 64 bytes, and the form queries its two halves apart. Of the form's 1,166,983 calls of the hash, 881,280 occur in `cp_verify`'s log, class by class, with the same index, length, start and answer: all 313,054 of seven classes, 568,210 of 853,901 for the rounds, and 16 of 28 of the configuration class (14 different queries, of which 8 occur there; that each is queried twice is inferred from these counts). `cp_verify`'s own log has 600,017 calls (one entry answers several calls of the form). The calls of the second hash function were not compared. Not in `cp_verify`'s log:
  - (a) Six hashes of the configuration class, each queried twice: with the index and length of calls of `cp_verify`, and other answers. These are calls of the form that is run, not queries of `verify`, and they are overwritten before anything reads them.
  - (b) Counted in different first halves for the rounds' challenges: the form queries 429,540. `cp_verify` computed 286,618 different calls: the form queries both halves of 281,592 of them, the first alone of 5,024 and the second alone of 2 (the last query of the key's and of the proof's shared chain). So there are 142,924 that `cp_verify` never computed.

    *The cause*, by reading and by these logs: Lean fetches a fixed number of blocks for each round (Fmt/Rules5.lean), and `cp_verify` computes a call only when the sampling reaches it. In 62,287 rounds `cp_verify`'s calls are a prefix of the form's, in 5,064 they are the same, and in 30 the log, whose order is that of evaluation, does not show it.

    *Whether a sample reads them:* with every half that `cp_verify` never computed answered by zeros (285,691 halves) the form's verdict on that proof was the same; with one call that `cp_verify` does compute zeroed as well, both halves, the verdict turned at each of the two places tried [evaluated]. That is less than "none is read": a reading of a zeroed answer that still let every test pass would look the same.

    *The other way:* `cp_verify` makes three calls that Lean never makes, one in each of its three processes, for the one digest of the parameter file alone, which nothing in `cp_verify` reads [read]; and it queries the chains' seven run values, which the form does not query.

    Why the form leaves out the second half of those 5,024 calls, each the last that `cp_verify` computed in its round, is not traced.
  - (c) *The order, a rejected proof, and the limits.* The form's log does not show the order of the calls. That is shown of `verify-on-bytes` alone (a Lean text: this repository builds no stand-alone program from it: "Terms used on this page"), evaluated as a whole at a logging hash on the same files and on the small example's: every chain call of `cp_verify` occurs in its log, in the same order within each of the three processes but for one jump in each; it asks 143,435 calls more at full size and 26,131 at the small, all of them tails of rounds. For each chain that the two logs show (eight by that comparison's count, which is not the count of this page's terms) the form's sequence of rounds, and of the chain's value before each round, is that text's (67,383 rounds, by another way of counting than (b)'s).

    On a copy of the small example's proof with one byte changed, `cp_verify` and that text both refuse, under other words, and stop at the same query [counted]; the form was put to no rejected proof in this comparison.

    The limits: one proof at each size, both stored examples, on synthetic weights, and one altered copy; Lean's logs hold no digest of a query's input, so that sameness rests on the answer beside number, index, length and start, and the tree's number is left out of the comparison; that the logging hash sees every query of the form rests on a reading [read]; the two comparisons count `cp_verify`'s chain calls differently, and 36 of the difference is not traced; no theorem says that no proof makes a test read further.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known.

### 23 · The tests that tie the two sides are few

- **The limit.** The tests are these: one row of the repository's test suite, of 22 calls on the two small examples, of which no run over this repository's files is told here; one script that runs the parser and then Lean's checks, in no suite; and at the full size runs of the form alone, of which only those on the two stored proofs can be repeated from this repository (14 (l), 45). No test compares the parser's reading with `cp_verify`'s. `cp_verify` has results that are neither accept nor reject, and it runs three processes where the format speaks of one session.
- **Layer.** The tie between Lean and the programs.
- **Why it matters.** A difference between the parser's reading and `cp_verify`'s would not be caught by a test.

### 25 · `verify` asks more than the C++ verifier

- **The limit.** The fourteen entry tests and two tests on the key file are added to `verify`, after the IOP's own tests on the plan. So `verify` could reject an honest proof that `cp_verify` accepts. The tests are one-sided: an added test can only turn an accept of `verify` into a reject (`LPFD.asksYes_append`, Johnson/Desc/Laid/AsksFirstLaw.lean:25: a list of tests that passes also passes with its last tests left off). That no test is taken out, weakened or moved is a rule in a comment (Johnson/Desc/Laid/ProofAsks.lean:14-15). Pin files that every build compiles prove that the lists written out in them are prefixes of the verifier's list (Johnson/Desc/Laid/ProofAsks.lean:16-23; `asksAt3_prefix`, Johnson/Desc/Laid/ProofAsksFirstFourteenTests.lean:78, and two shorter pins). A pin holds the tests by name: it does not see a change inside a test's own definition, and it fails only as long as it is not changed together with the list (Johnson/Desc/Laid/ProofAsksFirstFourteenTests.lean:5-8). Nothing here says that the two verifiers agree on any file.
  - Some such tests compare names or read a list with a default value, so that a slip in a name, or an empty list, would pass in silence.
  - Two silent default values on `verify`'s road (an empty request item; a rejection of the planner that is ignored) are taken to be unreachable in scope [read]. No named lemma says so.
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean: the tests and default values in `verify`'s text. The tie between Lean and the programs: whether the two verifiers agree.
- **Why it matters.** These defaults are no matter for the soundness theorem, which is about `verify` with them in its text. They bear on completeness.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known: no honest proof that `cp_verify` accepts was rejected at full size; the small examples' files are out of scope, and `verify` rejects them (section 1.2). The defaults found are not false at the stored files.

### 37 · No theorem says that the functions that build the request read the user's files as meant

- **The limit.** `Sound` speaks of the prompt and the output that the user hands to `verify`. The user's files become these arguments through two short Lean functions outside `verify`, `PV.Front.idOf` (Johnson/Top/Fire/Code/PvFront.lean:46) and `PV.Front.userOf` (Johnson/Top/Fire/Code/PvFront.lean:89); at the command `tools/lean-verify/lean_verify.sh` a third, `PV.Program.claimOf` (Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:102-107), calls the two. They stand under no statement. The claimed next token is the prover's message of one round, compared by the decision, not hashed first.
  - (a) No theorem says that these functions split the files as the format page means or as `cp_verify` does. The four theorems below take what `claimOf` returns as given, say nothing of the split, and state no soundness.
  - (b) No test compares what these functions read, `claimOf` or the two that it calls, with what `cp_verify` reads. The nearest test is indirect (below).
  - (c) No theorem speaks of `run` (Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:290), the Lean IO code that reads the files, prints the claim and evaluates the parts of `verdictOf` in turn [read] (Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:251-293), nor of the script and `LeanVerify.lean` around it, nor of code as it runs, interpreted or natively compiled. That the parts make `verdictOf` wherever `claimOf` returns a claim is a theorem of its own (`verdictOf_at`, Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:135); that the command calls them so is to be seen in the text of its function `ending` (Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:254).
  - (d) So the check of the soundness theorem's premise, that the three policy lines C, theta and cap read 256, 256 and 0, is the user's own (44).
  - (e) At this command, then, the gap is the text of `claimOf` and the two functions, some thirty lines with the library functions that they call, and the unproved code around them. So `callOk`, `claimOf` and what they call are to be checked by reading: `rangeOf`, `PV.Front.idOf` and `PV.Front.userOf`, and beneath these `words32`, `Fmt.unhex` and `Run.bytesToDig`.
- **Layer.** The tie between Lean and the programs: the Lean functions and the script that turn the user's files into the arguments of `verify`.
- **Why it matters.** A fault here would not affect the theorem. It could make the command print another verdict than the one `verify` gives, or judge another claim than the user's files state, so that the user checks another claim than they meant. This part of the interface is tested, not proved, and a run trusts what (45) names. So the command prints the claim that it judged, a second reader must print the same six lines of output, and the policy lines are the user's to check.
- **Proved nearest to it.** Eight theorems name the pure function `verdictOf` (Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:125), which takes the bytes of a call's eleven files (three public, the user's three, five from the parser outside Lean: 17) to a verdict or to none [counted: by written names]. Four are told below; the other four are `verdictOf_at` ((c)), `verdictOf_eq_finalVerifierProgram` (Johnson/Face/ExecutableVerifier.lean:40), `verdictOf_eq_executableVerifier` and `verdictOf_eq_leanVerifier` (AtFullSide/Face/ExecutableVerifierIsLeanVerifier.lean:60, :69; 39). No soundness statement is of `verdictOf` [counted: by written names]. The command runs with BLAKE2b in the oracle's place, where the soundness theorem draws that function at random [read].
  - Wherever `claimOf` returns a claim, `verdictOf` gives a verdict, and that verdict is the value of `verify` at exactly that claim, at the format's hash function (`verdictOf_eq`, Johnson/PVR/ProgramTotal.lean:43-45).
  - Where `claimOf` returns none, `verdictOf` gives none (`verdictOf_none`, Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:155).
  - One equation without a premise says both (`verdictOf_map`, Johnson/PVR/ProgramTotal.lean:37).
  - Any verdict that `verdictOf` gives is the value of `verify` at the claim that `claimOf` read (`verdictOf_is_verify`, Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:142-143).
- **Tested nearest to it.** How the command reads and prints the claim, and what it refuses: tools/lean-verify/README.md. The accept lines: 45 (f).
  - Of the policy lines, what follows is [read]; no lemma says it. The script takes the numbers from the line of the second reader (below), which at a verdict is Lean's own line, byte for byte (tools/lean-verify/lean_verify.sh:173-174, :295-300; Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:211-221, :276-277). At an accept the scope test of `verify` ties these lines to what its reader read: in scope the parameter file is, byte for byte, the stored text with the reader's numbers written into it (`InScope`, Ladder.lean:307-312). The same test fixes the parameter file's lines sh and sT at 8 and 4 (Ladder/Stored.lean:161-164), so at an accept the script's test of them can decide nothing.
  - `claimOf` calls the two functions only after tests 1 to 4 of docs/format/FORMAT.md, 12.2, have passed (`callOk`). A fifth test of the format's page, that every token is below the vocabulary's size, is not made by `callOk`: by its own comment the verifier makes it itself (Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:90-91). T is the constant 1,024 of the one size in scope, where that page takes T from the parameter file (Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:77-107).
  - 22 `#guard` lines stop the build that the command asks for unless `claimOf` answers on made-up files as they expect: five request files split, nine refused, two ID files read, six refused (Johnson/PVR/ProgramGuard.lean:77-100). They are tests at a few values, at every build [evaluated], not checked by Lean's kernel. The two that read an ID are a round trip: the ID that `claimOf` read is printed back in hex and compared with the digits of the file (Johnson/PVR/ProgramGuard.lean:68-70, :93-94), so a fault that reading and printing share would pass [read]. The command gives no verdict unless that build is there and, by Lake's records, fits the sources (tools/lean-verify/lean_verify.sh:125, :196).
  - A second reader, `read_claim.py`, written in Python from the format page, reads the claim first; it takes T from the parameter file and makes test 5 as well (tools/lean-verify/read_claim.py:79-94). The script gives no verdict unless its six lines of output and Lean's are the same, byte for byte (tools/lean-verify/lean_verify.sh:156-161, :275-280, :295-307; Johnson/PVR/Program.lean:267-278). Lean's lines meet that reader's only in a call, and only at full size [evaluated].
  - The nearest test against `cp_verify` is indirect: `tools/lean-verify/read_claim_test.py` compares the second reader's claim at the four stored examples with a line that `cp_verify` printed for them (the ID, the two positions r and e, the range of the checked tokens, whether the next token is claimed, the seven policy numbers (the format counts seven lines of the parameter file as the acceptance policy: C, theta, cap, s, sh, sT, F; this page's "policy" is the first three); not the tokens) [tested]. The two readers come from one project and one page, so their agreement says nothing of what `cp_verify` reads.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known.

### 57 · Honest public files can lie outside Lean's scope

- **The limit.** Honest fitted scale files (scale files made from a model's weights) exist that the Lean reader puts out of scope, for instance with rotary flags off the fixed pattern (35) [read]. At such files `verify` rejects, and the soundness theorem says nothing. Scope is a matter of the public files alone: whether the weights behind a fitted set meet the model's range conditions is not shown (54). (See also 62.)
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean: how far its scope reaches (35).
- **Why it matters.** It is a limit of completeness: an honest provider whose public files lie out of scope gets a rejection from `verify`.
- **Tested nearest to it.** One fitted set has been found to lie in scope: three public files of exact-match runs on real weights pass the scope test at exact match, and every security line of their parameter file is the default's [evaluated]. That is one evaluation, which cannot be repeated from this repository. The three files come from the same source folder as the files behind this repository's timed exact-match runs; the two sets were not compared byte for byte. Whether those real weights meet the model's range conditions is not shown (54). The gate (`protocolDataWellFormed`) was not evaluated at these files, none of the proofs of those runs on real weights was put to the executable form of `verify`, and the files of the timed runs under the other policy were not put to the test. The repository contains no fitted files.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known for soundness.

### 59 · Where the weights lie in the committed matrix

- **The limit.** That Lean's reading of the weights off the committed matrix matches the C++ prover's placement of them is not shown in Lean (Johnson/Flat/Claims.lean:38-40). It rests on the accept of the honest proof at the stored files alone [evaluated].
- **Layer.** The tie between Lean and the programs.
- **Why it matters.** A mismatch would not let a claim pass that is false of the weights as Lean reads them off the matrix. But those would then not be the weights that the C++ committer placed: a gap on which model the claim is about, of the same kind as 54.

### 66 · Bytes that the prover sends and `verify` never reads

- **The limit.** `verify` and `cp_verify` do not read the same bytes of a proof, and no theorem says what each reads. That an accepted proof cannot be altered into another accepted proof (non-malleability) is not claimed and is not proved of any verifier. For the same claim in other bytes it fails at the parsed form [evaluated] (14 (g)). Of the raw proof files nothing is proved either. The counts below are over the first stored set [counted: three programs].
  - (a) Of the proof's 99 trees two are opened twice. There the raw proof gives 276 digests (8,832 of 18,779,540 bytes; 250 at the second stored set [counted: once]) that the prover sends, that `cp_verify` hashes up to the root, and that `verify` never reads [counted]. At 189 of them `verify` computes the vertex from the other opening's leaves; the other 87 are a second copy [read]. `verifyOneProtocol`, the compiled verifier of one IOP and the arm of `verify`'s own `innerCompiledVerifier`, and the executable form's `verifyBSX` share this walk (Ladder.lean:411; Johnson/Top/InnerCompiledVerifier.lean:119-128; Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/FrontRootAns.lean:38-40; Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/CmsAns.lean:61-92).
  - (b) The second copy of the column and salt of three shared leaves (192 bytes) reaches `verify` once. Only the parser compares the copies [read].
  - (c) So `verify` and `cp_verify` disagree on bytes that the prover sent. `cp_verify` and the parser reject a raw proof in which one of the 276 is altered [read] (blocks/tree/tree.h:152-184; tools/parser/parse_files.py:338-342; tools/audit/cut.py:267-289), at four of them also [tested]. `verify` accepts the parsed file in which the matching path entry is altered [read] (14), at three such entries also [evaluated]. An altered file of a true claim is accepted because at the 189 the vertex is computed from opened leaves, at the 87 `verify` reads the other copy, and the root is met [read]. In the other 97 trees what is given and what is taken are the same set [read]. These readings say only why such files were accepted.
