Of the structure `FinalStatementOneBit` (Johnson/Face/FinalStatementOneBit.lean) two fields, `binding` and `repliesFit`, are two of the six lines; two, `sound` and `handsOutNoProof`, say what two of the lines say at more public files (`FilesTaken`: that the gate accepts them is not asked); `complete` is equivalent to `completeOnCanServe` (`finalComplete_iff_completeOn`, Johnson/Face/Checks.lean:57); and `zeroKnowledge` follows from two of the lines (`zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest_of_lines`) and is not proved to give `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` back. The structure with the label shown, `FinalStatement` (Johnson/Face/FinalStatement.lean), follows from it as a corollary (`finalStatement_of_oneBit`, AtFullSide/Face/TheFinalStatementOneBitHolds.lean).
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean: what the statements ask, not what the protocol does.
- **Why it matters.** All six lines, soundness among them, are proved of one system: the compiled system, `compile (flashProve setup)`, whose verifier is `verify`. Of the Lean verifier's own system, which has the same verifier and a constant honest side (55), only soundness is proved: one of the three properties that make up a doubly zero-knowledge commit-and-prove argument, and that one in a restricted form. For that system completeness cannot hold and zero knowledge would hold trivially, because its committer and its prover are constants (55 (a)) [paper].
- **Proved nearest to it.** `nearestSound_of_verify` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyAt.lean:78), the nearest form of `Sound`.
- **Known attack or counterexample.**
  - `SoundOpened` and `SoundAndBinding` as written cannot hold at a small error, by theorems, given completeness under a policy and the files as read (G3'pol, `FilesRead`; `packageSoundOpened_bar`, Ladder/PackageTies.lean:416; `packageSoundAndBinding_bar`, Ladder/PackageTies.lean:434).
  - `Sound` at all files in scope is not expected to hold at a small error [paper] (Ladder/Package.lean:86-91).
  - `Complete` cannot hold of any IOP that meets the goal on soundness under a policy (G8, Ladder.lean), since that goal counts an accepted empty output as a forgery (Ladder.lean:188-191).
  - `ZeroKnowledge` is not expected to hold as written either: whether a request can be served depends on the weights, so what the honest prover does at a request that cannot be served tells something of them (the comment on `PackageZeroKnowledge`, Ladder/Package.lean:177-178).
  - None of this is a fault of the protocol. For the three statements on soundness it is due to the goals' family of files, which contains policies other than exact match (Ladder/Package.lean:36-39). For completeness and zero knowledge it is a fault of the statements, which speak of every request (Ladder/Package.lean:40-43).

### 10 · Index binding, correctness of extraction, binding of the commitment and no two openings are four things

- **The limit.** Of `verify` with no committer named only (a) is proved. (b) and (c), and with them "one ID, one set of weights" in the sense of the committer's own weights, need an honest committer's text in Lean. In the Lean verifier's own system that text is the constant committer's (55); of the compiled system, whose committer is `compile`'s own, (b) is `finalExtracts_value`, and (c) is the line `soundAndBinding`, with binding at the named extractor and no two openings in `bindingOf_value` (AtFullSide/Face/FinalBindingAtTheValue.lean; 39). Of `cp_commit` nothing is proved.
  - (a) Index binding is what `Sound` states: the commitment ID, the committer's hash queries and the public files (69) fix one set of weights, the extractor's, or none, before any proof, and an accepted output is the model's at these weights, but for the error (many proofs: 70). The name does not mean that a commitment stands for the same weights under all public files.
  - (b) Correctness of extraction: that these are the weights which an honest committer put in.
  - (c) Binding of the commitment, in the opening form: that nobody opens one ID to weights other than the extractor's; and no two openings: that nobody opens one ID to two sets of weights.
  - In the goals' game the index verification key is the 256-bit commitment ID alone (VerifiableInference/Statements.lean:30-31). The whole key file is the prover's, and its one-time proof is part of every typed proof (Ladder.lean:339-341; Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/FrontRoot.lean:43-60). The key file is ivk/key.bin: in spite of its folder's name it is no part of the index verification key in Lean's statements. So what fixes the weights is the ID with the committer's hash queries and the public files (69). In that game no theorem speaks of a key file by itself, and none says that a key file that passes fixes the weights.
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean: (a), the key in the goals' game, and that (b) and (c), though asked of the Lean verifier's own system (Ladder/Package.lean:114-148), are false or vacuous of it [paper] (55 (a)). The tie between Lean and the programs: that no illustrative committer is tied to `cp_commit`.
- **Why it matters.** The possible consequences of a gap here: (64).
- **Proved nearest to it.** Index binding, of `verify`: the soundness theorem (28), by `sound_iff` (VerifiableInference/Agreement.lean:232), and by name `indexBinding_of_verify_at` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyForms.lean:341). Of the compiled verifier `innerCompiledVerifier` it follows from the 15 hypotheses of the theorem on `innerCompiledVerifier`, `innerCompiledVerifier_sound` (Johnson/Top/InnerCompiledVerifier.lean:299), that name no claim (`CompilerSoundnessConditions.binds`, Johnson/Top/InnerCompiledVerifier.lean:264). `Rom.binding_overOne` (Rom/ExitBind.lean:117; section 2.4) speaks of index binding in this sense.
  - Of illustrative committers, tied to none of the programs by a theorem: 55's table, 64, 72. Only one has been compared with a program, in values alone (55, row `theCpp`; section 1.2).
  - That the offline IOP is sound round by round is a theorem one level below (2).

### 6 · What the theorem under a policy does not say

- **The limit.** The theorem under a policy, `soundUnderPolicy_of_verify_at` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyPolicy.lean:63; section 2.2), holds at every acceptance policy in scope. It means something only together with three things that are not proved of the Lean verifier's system (b). That tolerant outputs are accepted is not proved ((e)); at one policy other than exact match accepts of honest proofs have been seen [evaluated] (14). Whether a policy is a good test is no matter of Lean's.
  - (b) The theorem under a policy means something only together with: correctness of extraction at the same extractor (that the extracted weights are the ones an honest committer put in), which cannot be stated with any content of the Lean verifier's system, whose committer is a constant one; knowing who made the commitment, which no theorem says; and completeness. The goal on soundness under a policy (`G8`) means something only together with the goal on opened commitments (`G9`) and the goal on completeness under a policy (`G3'pol`) (Ladder.lean:574-575), and of this IOP neither is proved (`G9`: 55). Where correctness of extraction is proved at that extractor, and the pair of `G8bits` and `G9bits` at an illustrative system: 55's table. Of the Lean verifier's system the pair is not written.
  - (c) By itself it bars nothing against a committer who commits to weights under which every output meets the policy. All-zero weights, which are in range, meet every policy with theta and C of at least 257, at every non-empty prompt and output within the conditions of `meetsPolicy_zeroWeights` (Policy/Front/TrueUnder.lean:309), so a theorem must name its extractor.

    In general a statement of this shape says as much as its claim says, and the claim is part of what a reader has to read: a claim that some weights meet at every prompt and output makes the form that names no extractor (`SoundFor`, VerifiableInference/MoreStatements.lean:64) true of every argument system, with ε = 0 [paper]. Under many tolerant policies some such choice of weights lets every well-formed output meet the policy, under side conditions. Some policies within the bounds leave every counted token free (44).

    The theorem speaks of the weights that its extractor reads off the commitment and the committer's hash queries at the given public files, whoever made the commitment.
  - (d) Another way, well-formedness of the extracted index (TERMS.md), is not at hand either: this library defines no such predicate beside the two optional additions of ZKCP/Optional.lean (ZKCP/Defs.lean:12-13).
  - (e) It does not show that what `verify` accepts moves with the policy: a verifier that enforced exact match everywhere would meet it too. That tolerant outputs are accepted is `G3'pol`, which is not proved.
  - (f) What it scores. It reads `Policy.MeetsPolicy`, whose mean and count are taken over one output, which the prover picks. Only output tokens inside the window of 1,024 tokens of prompt and output are counted, and the prompt's tokens are not scored; an output token after a full window must be the model's next token, and prompt and output have at most 1,025 tokens together (Policy/Front/MeetsPolicy.lean:117-118; `HasScores.length_le`, Policy/Front/MeetsPolicy.lean:106). So a reply that is cut into several outputs has a mean and a count in each, and what is scored in one is unscored prompt in the next (Policy/Front/MeetsPolicy.lean:85-90, :136-139). The prover picks the number of counted tokens.
  - (g) A token's cost is in the unit of the committer's own weights: whoever makes the weights sets their scale (Policy/Front/MeetsPolicy.lean:59-61). It is no rank and no probability.
  - (h) "Meets the policy" is this library's own definition, not the published tolerance test, and the tolerance test is not proved. `indexBindingUnderPolicy_of_verify_at` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyForms.lean:401) holds at all policies in scope, at the same error, for another relation: the output meets the files' policy. It is not `HasIndexBinding` of the model's own relation, and it does not mean that Token-DiFR's test is proved.
  - (i) Part of the goal on soundness under a policy does not rule out an extractor that always returns the all-zero weights: such an extractor meets it, and only a statement at the same extractor rules it out: the goal on opened commitments (`G9`, Ladder.lean, point 6) or correctness of extraction. Neither `G9` nor completeness under a policy is proved of the Lean verifier's system, whose honest committer and prover are constant ones; of the illustrative system on byte strings correctness of extraction is, at files at which `protocolDataWellFormed` passes. Its conditions: 55, row `theCpp`. The part of the goal meant here speaks of policies with theta and C of at least 257, and such an extractor meets it with a verifier that only tests the form of a request (Ladder.lean:104-106). At that illustrative system the body of `G9bits` is a theorem too, under the same condition, which is not the goal itself (72) (`bindsAt`, Johnson/Bind/Cpp/TheTie.lean:216-223), and `G9` and `G9bits` whole are theorems of another illustrative system (`G9_digits`, `G9bits_digits`, Johnson/Goal/Conditions/WideDigits.lean:181-183; 55, row `theWideDigits`).
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean.
- **Why it matters.** Under a tolerant policy an accept says less than at exact match, and it looks the same (44). A reader who takes such an accept for "the output is the model's" assumes more than the theorem gives. It weighs on the intended use.
- **Tested nearest to it.** At one policy other than exact match, accepts of honest proofs have been seen, and two rejections of replies that came labelled as false under that policy (beyond its limits) [evaluated]. Each rejection names a place in the proof, not the policy: no test on the proof's values is the policy's own. So no rejection is shown to be due to the tolerance itself (14, 76).

### 16 · Where Lean is silent, only a technical report that is not public speaks

- **The limit.** No theorem that Lean proves is affected here. Where Lean is silent, all that speaks is a technical report on this protocol that is not public, and that is not in this repository [paper]. No Lean statement rests on a theorem of the technical report: where a file follows it, the tie is [read] (LIMITS.md, L48; CREDITS.md). This entry rests on docs/limits.md and on the technical report, not on anything in this folder.
  - (a) The standing of the technical report: statements of it have been found false as stated; not every fault that is known in it is claimed to be corrected; no complete parameter set has been shown to meet all the conditions of its theorems; it lists places as open for soundness.
  - (b) The count behind the target of 128 bits a round: the bounds have the form of the published analysis on which the protocol builds (ePrint 2026/391, version of February 25, 2026, Lemmas 7.4 and 9.9), which proves them for that paper's constructions. That they hold for the protocol of the technical report is not proved [paper].
  - (c) docs/limits.md tells, beside the figure for one round, a lower one counted jointly for a full batch, and says that it is not known which of the two applies to a single request.
  - (d) The programs are not the protocol of the technical report: they differ.
- **Layer.** None of the three: the protocol on paper, where Lean is silent. The programs: at (d).
- **Why it matters.** It affects no theorem that Lean proves. The figures that docs/limits.md gives for a round depend on it (b, c).
- **Known attack or counterexample.** No attack known. Whether two published attacks on proofs made non-interactive by hashing apply has not been examined (docs/limits.md).

### 40 · No theorem carries a statement between the compiler's two forms

- **The limit.** The compiler turns an interactive oracle proof into an argument by hashing. It has two forms: the library's own (Compile/Batch/), and a second form of its verifier's side alone, which differs from it in five places (OnBytes/TypedOracleCompiler/; the five: OnBytes/TypedOracleCompiler/Compile/Batch/Verifier.lean:7-19). `verify` is built with the second. No theorem of OnBytes/TypedOracleCompiler/ carries a statement from one form to the other (OnBytes/TypedOracleCompiler/Compile/Batch/Verifier.lean:21-22).
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean.
- **Why it matters.** A theorem stated with one form does not reach the other. What that leaves for hiding, index privacy among it, is told at 15 (8). It does not affect the soundness theorem (28): the second form, with which `verify` is built, has its own road to soundness (OnBytes/TypedOracleCompiler/Compile/Batch/Verifier.lean:6-7, a comment), and the joining of a proof's three chains is a theorem inside that theorem's proof (section 2.4).

### 41 · The copy's number is a label that an accept does not certify; two OOD samples

- **The limit.**
  - (a) A copy is a further encoding of the same weights; the copy's number says which stored copy a proof is made against. `num`, a field of `OutOfDomainSampleQueryLayout` (Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/Seeds.lean:83), is a free field, under no law: the number that a proof names is a label that an accept does not certify. The field belongs to the compiler that `verify` itself calls (Ladder.lean:411; Johnson/Top/InnerCompiledVerifier.lean:76, :121, :128; its verifier `verifyAtKeyRoot` is `verifyAtFullKey`: Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/FrontRootAns.lean:38-40), and that verifier's accept begins with the field's test, `num` applied to the number that the proof names and to the proof's plain parts (Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/CmsAns.lean:91).
    - By the library's comments alone, which no theorem states: the test is meant to ask whether the proof contains the number at the place where the first query of the session's chain includes it (Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/Seeds.lean:81-82); no proof uses the test, which can only make `VC` reject more (Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/Cms.lean:61-62: said of `VC`, in a file limited to index binding, :54; it holds of `verifyAtFullKey` by the same conjunction); and the number tested is the prover's free choice, tied by nothing to the copy's file picked (Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/Cms.lean:104-105).
    - At the small-size checks nothing holds the copy's number against the number in the copy's file's name (Ask/AllCompiledSmall.lean:65-66) [read]; root and answers are held to the commitment ID in the run that joins the three files' checks (Ask/AllCompiledSmall.lean:48-53) [read]. In a parsed file the fields `root`, `answers` and `copy` are the parser's claim, which whoever uses them has to hold against the commitment ID (OnBytes/Maker/Carrier.lean:379-382) [read].
  - (b) The soundness of the interactive protocol of Hiop/RecInterface.lean (`SoundR`), five separate clauses, does not by its form rule out two OOD samples at one committed table (Hiop/RecInterface.lean:25, 42-43).
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean.
- **Why it matters.** No false statement about the copy's root passes by the number alone. What the link's soundness gives toward that, with its error and its two other arms (the first word does not decode; the further word has a collision), is at (34), which alone ties nothing down.
- **Proved nearest to it.** At the parameters `homeT b Z` the test is proved to compare the number with the one in the plain part of the proof's own front round, the proof file's first round (`num_homeT`, Johnson/Goal/Conditions/HomeNumFront.lean:40-44, by `rfl`). That these are the `LayoutsByPublicReading` value of `verify`'s own system rests on `fits_descriptionOfFields` (Johnson/Flat/Told/Dec/LayoutsAtDescriptionOfFields.lean:55) and `T_wider` (Johnson/Goal/Conditions/HomeAtWider.lean:112-113). So, at those parameters, `verify` tests the number against the proof's own front round alone. The format's rule on the number is R14.2 of docs/format/.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** At (b), none known.

### 20 · `HasIndexPrivacy` asks less than the definition of that name

- **The limit.** `HasIndexPrivacy` (ZKCP/Defs.lean:420) asks less than the definition that this library follows (CREDITS.md; the appendix, "The twelve parts in the terms of the definition"), in spite of its name: in it, whoever picks the requests sees none of the verifier's hash queries [read]. In `HasStrongIndexPrivacyWith` (ZKCP/Defs.lean:454) that party sees the verifier's whole view, as the definition asks, but for the two things told in the appendix [read]. `ZeroKnowledge` and `ZeroKnowledgeOn` are typed the second way. The comments in ZKCP/Defs.lean (:428-430, :446-448), and in the files that follow it, call `HasIndexPrivacy` the literal definition or the literal form, and `HasStrongIndexPrivacyWith` the stronger form; where they and this page differ on that, this page takes precedence.
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean.
- **Why it matters.** It affects no statement on soundness. The definitions to read for what is proved are `Sound`, `CanServe`, `MeetsPolicy` and `modelNextToken`, and beside these those of the six lines (39): among them `CompleteOn`, `ZeroKnowledgeOn`, `ZeroKnowledgeBelowWhere`, `requestOutcome`, `WellFormedRequest` and, for the corollary with the label (`zeroKnowledgeWithLabel`), `ZeroKnowledgeShownBelowWhere` and `requestLabel`.
- **Proved nearest to it.** A made-up scheme meets `HasIndexPrivacy` and fails `HasStrongIndexPrivacyWith` (`leaky_private`, ZKCP/Separation.lean:199; `leaky_not_strong`, ZKCP/Separation.lean:300).

### 15 · Weight hiding in Lean

- **The limit; proved in part.** Zero knowledge is proved in Lean for the protocol, of the honest provers written in Lean: honest-verifier zero knowledge of the IOP before hashing, and the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe`, with its corollary `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest`, after compiling, in the random-oracle model, for one commitment with one copy of the table (39). Not covered: batches, coins derived from a short key, the clipped-gap policy, a second commitment, what the public files themselves tell of the weights, timing, the simulators' running time; and one bit for each request (served or refused) is revealed, and it depends on the weights (in a second corollary, `zeroKnowledgeWithLabel`, at well-formed requests, one label: served, failed or other tokens).

  Of the appendix's twelve parts these theorems meet (1) but for the OOD answers as the programs make them, (2) but for the last sentence of its field "The limit", (8) for the IOP that `verify` runs, and of (5) a rejected request. (3), (4), (6), (9), (12) and the rest of (5) are open; (7), (10) and (11) stand as they are told there. Of the programs nothing is proved (section 1).
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean. For the programs and the written proof: guide/weight-hiding.md.
- **Why it matters.** The soundness theorem (28) says nothing on hiding, and this gap affects no statement on soundness. A theorem of one object here is no theorem of another.
- **Proved nearest to it.**
  - Of the honest provers written in Lean, before hashing and after compiling: 39.
  - Of `theDescription`, with fixed-message provers, fresh random masks and one request to a proof, what is shown of the committed tables can be simulated without the weights, up to a stated error, in an argument other than `verify`'s own. One conditional theorem carries any three provers through hashing, and 15 of its 20 conditions, those that name no prover, coin or simulator, hold at `verify`'s own input (a count of conditions: it does not weigh them). Before hashing, the link proof's honest prover has honest-verifier zero knowledge. Each by its Lean name, with its limits: the appendix's head.

### 75 · The conditional theorem on index privacy: further limits

- **The limit; proved in part.** The line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` (39), and so its corollary, is derived from `OnBytes.WholeKey.wholeKeyPrivacy`: inside the line's proof that theorem's 20 conditions (told below) are met by the protocol's own three provers, made to refuse a request that is not served, at public files in use alone and at one copy of the table, not at up to 710,324 copies as in the results with fixed-message provers [read: once, names followed in code]. No theorem states that by name: the value of `PremW`, the structure whose 20 fields are that theorem's 20 conditions (Johnson/WholeKey/Premises.lean:358-397), is built in `compiled_indexPrivacy` (Johnson/T3/CompiledPrivacy.lean:68), for any provers, and the line's proof calls it at the protocol's [read].

  Three of the 20 come from the theorem on the IOP beneath it (`zkOnServedRequests_inScope`), and stand as far as that stands. One, on the run values, is shown at public files in use alone; one comes from the settings' laws on coins; fourteen from `flash_forIndexPrivacy` (Johnson/CompletenessAndZeroKnowledge/PublicAtFlash.lean:56); the last, on the OOD seeds, is met inside the proof, since none is set aside [read].

  The provers' term contains provers that send fixed messages, as the branches that it takes, offline and online, where a function that makes them refuses; that these branches are not taken at public files in use is a theorem for the offline branch (`offlineDescription_named`, `offlineProver_eq`); for the online branch it is read off three theorems that no one theorem joins (`k1_sim_at_term`: `onlineMaker`, the function from whose return the online prover is made, returns; `head_of_live`: "the head", the condition `AtMgA` on the public files, has a member; `online_prover_at_head`: under the head, where `onlineMaker` returns, the online prover takes its first branch). Its offline senders contain default values, and that none of them matters in scope is not shown (39, "The limits", (b)).

  `OnBytes.WholeKey.wholeKeyPrivacy` says that 20 conditions give index privacy after hashing, up to an error given by a formula (Johnson/WholeKey/Main.lean:93; statement: Johnson/WholeKey/Statement.lean:163).
  - (a) The conditions on the simulators are strict. Each simulator's output must have exactly the honest output's distribution, at every value of the encoding randomness and every OOD seed of a public set, except on a set of verifier messages of bounded measure.
  - (b) Its key is the whole public key, and by this theorem itself nothing is said of a verifier that first reads parameters off the hash function: 15 (8).
  - (c) It is shown at two toys, small made-up protocols written in Lean alone, whose index is not weights (`pad`, Hiop/Seeded/Pad.lean:71; `pad2`, Hiop/Seeded/Pad2.lean:346). At both, three of the error's five parts are zero: the simulators' errors, the stops, the OOD seeds. No salted leaf hides anything there that the toy's one-time pad does not hide already [read].
  - (d) An error below 1 shows little, for completeness as for hiding; what counts is the numbers. At the second toy: a completeness error of at most 1/16, for one instance, and a privacy error of at most 1/4 + 204/2^32, at one hash query and three sessions. At full size the error is at most 2^-135 for fixed-message provers, with the conditions told at the head of 15's appendix; for the protocol's own provers, at one copy of the table, the error of the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` is at most 2^-154, within its limits (39). Provers of the second toy that send fixed messages are accepted by chance with probability 15/128, so their completeness error is 113/128, below 1, and they are private [paper]. No theorem says that provers that send fixed messages fail completeness.
  - (e) The error's five parts have nine summands in all. Two of them, the term for the sessions' simulation errors and the term for the sessions' stops, have a test that would fail if they were removed; the other seven have none, and nothing tests the budgets. No example is shown in which a simulation error, a stop or an OOD seed outside the public set has probability above 0 and the error is below 1/2.
- **Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean.
- **Why it matters.** What Lean proves on hiding after hashing rests on this theorem (39) [read]. As typed it is not about the argument system whose verifier is `verify`: 15 (8).
- **Proved nearest to it.** For the protocol as Lean describes it: 15 of the 20, those that name no prover, coin or simulator (a count of conditions: it does not weigh them); all 20 with fixed-message provers; all 20 with the link proof's honest prover beside two fixed-message provers (the head of 15's appendix); and, inside a proof, all 20 by the protocol's own three provers ("The limit", above) [read]. At the two toys of (c): `OnBytes.WholeKey.private_pad` and `OnBytes.WholeKey.private_pad2` (Johnson/WholeKey/Main.lean:212, :250) compare the real experiment with the ideal one at each index of the range. Two real experiments against each other, at the same toys: `OnBytes.WholeKey.real_pad` and `OnBytes.WholeKey.real_pad2` (Johnson/WholeKey/Half.lean:75, :95), by `OnBytes.WholeKey.real_real` (Johnson/WholeKey/Half.lean:56).
  - `OnBytes.WholeKey.FoeScheme.parts_bite` and `OnBytes.WholeKey.FoeScheme.stops_bite` (Johnson/WholeKey/FoeZeta.lean:296, :364): without its term for the sessions' simulation errors, or for the sessions' stops, the statement would be false. Both test their term at the value 1, where the error is 1 or more and the theorem says nothing: they show that the terms are needed, not that they are tight.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known in what it says.

### (no number) · Outside the scope, or of small account

Six matters are outside the scope or of small account:

- whose weights were committed;
- that tokens are numbers;
- a classical adversary;
- that Lean's kernel and compilers are trusted;
- what the public scale file, which at a fitted model is made from the weights, tells of them (counted by no statement on hiding: the docstring of `PackageZeroKnowledge`, Ladder/Package.lean:173-174; what it tells: docs/what-is-not-hidden.md, item 5);
- one line of the parameter file, which has two bounds in two readers of that file (256 and 2^20; every goal fixes it at 64).

LIMITS.md has the first four.

**Not a gap for soundness.** No verifier compares the batch's shared proximity test across the files of one batch. Index binding is a statement about the verifier of one request (10); two files that claim one batch and carry different shared tests are, for the theorem, two batches. That at most N requests stand in front of one shared test is typed, as the field `slots` of `QueryLayout.OKG` (OnBytes/TypedOracleCompiler/Iop/BatchWire.lean:284-289), which the hypothesis `wires` asks (13, 3). No page states the order of hash queries across a batch's requests.

## 5. Settled points, and numbers that are given under another entry

"Settled" means: as far as the soundness theorem (28) stands, that is, checked by Lean's kernel. Where an entry is tagged [read], [counted] or [evaluated], it is settled by that alone. An entry that contains open matter says so in its first words. What a settled entry leaves open follows its first line, by number.

### 1 · The online IOP is sound round by round

Settled: `proofSoundAt_of_verify` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyMore.lean:69).

`proofSound` holds at the protocol as Lean describes it, at public files in scope that are read (at which term it is typed: 13, 3, the table). It asks something only where the gate accepts (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/TopAtZ.lean:91-93). That the gate accepts is proved only at the written copy, and at that copy with only the policy changed (73). At the two IOPs of Johnson/Flat/TwoSizes.lean, at which section 4 tells the theorem on `innerCompiledVerifier`, `innerCompiledVerifier_sound` (13, 3; TERMS.md), it is open.

### 2 · The offline IOP is sound round by round

Settled: `keySound_holds` (Johnson/Goal/Conditions/KeyOpenHolds.lean:87), with no hypothesis, on no axiom beyond Lean's three standard ones, with all 21 laws of the key file included.

`keySound` is stated for the relation: the word has a collision at the OOD seed, or decodes with the OOD sample (Johnson/Top/InnerCompiledVerifier.lean:154-159). So a collision is one of the two ways to meet it. It is one of three statements beneath the soundness theorem, not that theorem (at which term it is typed: 13, 3, the table). It rests on the coding theory of 12; see also 63. It asks something only at public files in scope at which the gate accepts. That the gate accepts is proved only at the written copy, and at that copy with only the policy changed (73).

A law speaks only at a statement inside its standing conditions at which its own sub-protocol accepts. One such statement has been seen for each of the 21, over four runs, not all over one state of the library, by files that are not in this repository [evaluated]: one stored honest view at the full size, synthetic weights, oracles and transcripts constructed for the evaluation, not from a run of the prover (Johnson/Goal/Conditions/KeyOpenHolds.lean:24-40). That shows that each law applies to at least one statement, and nothing more: not why the proximity tests' errors lie below the bound written into the laws. One of the 21 declares no round, so there an accept is the step's decision alone.

How it stands in the files of section 4: (13, 3), the table.

### 34 · The link IOP is sound round by round

Settled: `entrySound` (Johnson/Goal/Conditions/EntrySits.lean:52; statement: Johnson/Top/InnerCompiledVerifier.lean:160-166).

It is a theorem about the very decision that `verify` calls: an interactive protocol's round-by-round soundness, at the full size, up to its rounds' errors. It rests on the coding theory of 12 (`Cb.Gl.bchks46At`, Cb/Bridge/GlCodesProved.lean:20). Its relation: the first word does not decode, or the further word has a collision, or both decode to the same index. Two of the three arms are met with no link at all, and the bound counts proof of work (36 (f)). By itself it ties nothing down: it needs (2); and a decision that never accepts would meet it. How it stands in the files of section 4: (13, 3), the table.

### 8 · The level depends on tests that `verify` itself runs on public data

Settled: `VerifierAndGoals.AtTheName.sound_of_verify_at` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyAt.lean:69).

The theorem derives the level from these tests' accept. Beyond the three conditions in the statement (in scope, read without error, exact match: 44), no test on the public files is assumed that `verify` does not run itself (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfBareLawsDoor.lean:23-24). For the proof file the planner runs with its own security check switched off, so the level there rests on these tests being inside `verify`. That they accept is proved only at the written copy, and at that copy with only the policy changed (73).

### 24 · A mistake shared with `cp_verify` that harmed soundness within what the theorem states would show in the proof

Settled: `VerifierAndGoals.AtTheName.sound_of_verify_at` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyAt.lean:69).

The decisions inside the Lean IOP are written by hand after the C++ verifier: a mistake shared with `cp_verify` that harmed soundness would show in the proof, within what the theorem states (exact match, files in scope). See 58.

### 29 · The soundness theorem's error has no factor for a batch of up to 64 requests

Settled: `k14_at_free_head` (Johnson/Desc/K14/Law/LedgerAtFreeHead.lean:101).

The shared chain's law is asked for a batch of as many requests as the scope allows and is derived inside the theorem, under the state that the gate establishes; that file claims no example. Of the 59 rounds that a batch shares 36 have less than 2 bits of room above 128; a factor of 64 would have cost 6 (on the 59 rounds: 12, "The fit is sharp").

### 30 · The theorem has no hypothesis on any round's error

Settled: `VerifierAndGoals.AtTheName.sound_of_verify_at` (Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/SoundOfVerifyAt.lean:69).

Each law carries its rounds' bound at the cap on a round's error (section 2.4, 28 (c)). The fit is sharp: the 25 rounds of the approximate range proof have no room at all (of other rounds: 12, "The fit is sharp").

### 31 · That no concrete hash function is reached under `verify`

Part of 51. That entry is [read] and [counted], no theorem.

### 32 · The theorem's closed hypotheses hold at the written copy

Settled, at the written copy alone: the gate accepts there (`theLive_zFull`, AtFullSide/Top/LiveAtFull.lean:60; with only the policy changed: `theLive_zGap`, AtFullSide/Top/LiveAtPolicies.lean:51), and the files are in scope (`VerifierAndGoals.inScope_full`, Johnson/Goal/Files/ScopeExample.lean:31).

The theorem has no hypothesis that could be empty but the three closed ones of its statement (8), which hold, by theorem, at the written copy `VerifierAndGoals.storedPublicFiles` (73). The further closed conditions that its proof needs follow from the gate's accept; that they pass is proved at the written copies and is evaluated or unknown elsewhere (73). An accept of `protocolDataWellFormed` is no sign that `verify` accepts any proof. What it leaves open: (52, 73, 14).

In a trial of all closed conditions of another form of the theorem, not repeated for this one, one line of 261 was not as expected (a negative test under which the condition passed), 26 conditions had no negative test, and 20 had no code [evaluated].

### 38 · From two words to many rows

Open, as part of (12).

### 43 · Two tests on public data alone, outside the theorem's assumptions

Settled as assumptions: one is run inside `verify`, and the other is derived. Open: `rowsRead`, a condition on the public files (the rows of what is sent can be made from them, and in the first proof chain's rows one shape has one radius: Johnson/Goal/Skeleton/RowsRead.lean:5-6), is what the fourteenth entry test provides (48): it rests on that test's accept, and it is not proved for all files in scope. In its negative test, of 167 altered radii 157 were rejected, and under the other ten the test passed [evaluated].

### 47 · Part of 49

### (no number) · A sampled position out of range, at the decision on the copy's file

Open: it is stated at 79 (section 3.3).

### (no number) · Four constant decisions in the statement's reach

No gap. Three always accept and one always rejects [counted]. None stands in a test's place, and none is reached from the decisions of `verify` [read].

## Appendix. Gap 15, weight hiding

This appendix tells what Lean's statements on hiding say, of which object and under which hypotheses, and which statements are missing, can be met trivially or are typed for another object. For the programs and the written proof: guide/weight-hiding.md. What is proved, with its bounds: gap 39; THEOREM.md; TERMS.md. In this appendix (1) to (12) are its own parts; a gap of the page is written with the word gap, as in gap 55.

**Layer.** The protocol as written in Lean. Where a part says "as run", it states where the programs differ from what Lean assumes: the tie between Lean and the programs.

**Proved, of the honest provers written in Lean.** Honest-verifier zero knowledge of the IOP, before hashing, and the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe`, with its corollary `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest`, after compiling: gap 39, with each one's scope and limits.

**Proved beside these, with fixed-message provers, of the protocol as Lean describes it.**

- Against any verifier, what is shown of the committed tables (the encoded weights), the published masked sums (the out-of-domain (OOD) answers) included, can be simulated without the weights, up to a stated error. That is with fixed-message provers, with fresh random masks, and for one request to a proof. It follows from the conditional theorem below, so it is of that theorem's argument, not of `verify`'s own system. The programs derive their masks from a secret seed, which no Lean theorem covers.
- Lean has one conditional theorem: 20 conditions give index privacy after hashing, up to an error given by a formula (`OnBytes.WholeKey.wholeKeyPrivacy`, Johnson/WholeKey/Main.lean:93; statement: Johnson/WholeKey/Statement.lean:163). It is stated for any three provers, against any verifier within a bound on its hash queries.
  - Before hashing and for an honest verifier, the way the three parts compose is a conditional theorem too, for any provers that have the encoder's tables and have simulators of their own (`AtPublicFiles.AtFiles.whole_atFiles`, E2E/WholeViewAtFiles.lean:191). It asks no acceptance of the provers, and its premises are shown of the fixed-message provers alone.
- Fifteen of the 20 are proved for the protocol as Lean describes it, a count of conditions that does not weigh them (`AtPublicFiles.Premises.noProverSide_description`, E2E/PremisesAtFiles.lean:410). The other five speak of the three honest provers (key file, link proof, request), their simulators and coins: it is these five that ask hiding itself.
  - Of the 15, eight follow from the protocol's written data and the library's lemmas, two are arithmetic on the chosen numbers, and five hold by type or by default. One of those five holds because no OOD seed is set aside; if the prover's side has to set some aside, the count is 14. One of the 15 holds because the honest prover's number of copies is supposed to be at most 710,324. One reads the encoder's random values, as a uniform distribution. One is the statement for a single table, and every limit of the result on the committed tables (1) goes with it.
  - The 15 hold up to 710,324 copies and 688,127 sessions a copy, of one request each; of batches of two or more nothing is said. These are the most that fit the budget of 44,040,191 columns a table (62 + 62 · 710,324 = 44,040,150; 62 + 64 · 688,127 = 44,040,190). The counts 62 and 64 are upper bounds: that the verifiers ever read so many is not shown. By the programs' own accounting a copy serves at most 688,127 batches of up to 64 requests; no theorem ties that count to Lean's sessions.
  - They hold at all public files of the one proof format and model size that `verify` takes. Where `verify`'s own tests on these files fail, what they say of the verifiers is vacuous.
- Fixed-message provers meet all 20. With them, at full size, where the tests on the public files pass, the error is at most 2^-135 (one copy, one request, 2^64 hash queries), and proofs are produced under the proof-of-work limits of that bound, 2^40 + 1 tries a search (`AtPublicFiles.ZetaAt.privacyWithNumber_description`, E2E/ZetaAtCaps.lean:238; that proofs are produced: E2E/StringsInGame.lean, E2E/SessionsInGame.lean). For more copies and requests the bound is a formula, which no theorem evaluates there. The theorems that bound the error ask at least 256 free bits in a leaf's salt; the figure stands at 320 of a salt's 576 bits. With no free bit the error is 1 or more [paper].
- That result holds too, at full size, where the tests on the public files pass, with the link proof's honest prover, as written in Lean, beside two fixed-message provers, at one fixed public-files digest, at which `verify` is not proved ever to be called (`AtPublicFiles.LinkLaid.linkLaidAtOnce`, E2E/LinkLaidPrivacy.lean:448). On these tests: gaps 52 and 73. There the public bits of the salts are zeros by choice. That the hashed verifier accepts, even where proof of work passes, is not proved.
- In both results the key file's and the request's provers send fixed messages: nothing that these two would compute from the weights is covered. Fixed messages hide the weights trivially, so on the provers' own messages a result on hiding counts only beside completeness of the same provers.
- The link proof's prover also has perfect honest-verifier zero knowledge before hashing, at the library's full-size parameter set, with no premise, with an exact simulator that is given the two tables' OOD samples (seeds and answers), the verifier's messages and the opened columns, at most 62 of each table (`OnBytes.LinkSim.AtHome.homeExactAt`, Johnson/LinkSim/AtHome/HomeExactAll.lean:123). This is not a statement that the weights are hidden: answers and columns are computed from them.

**Not covered by the results with fixed-message provers:** the proofs' own messages, in the proof inside the commitment's key file and in the proof sent for each request: it is of these that hiding itself is asked (the five conditions above). The theorems of gap 39, which are about the honest provers written in Lean, speak of these messages; that these provers meet the five conditions is stated by no theorem; it is shown inside a proof [read] (gap 75).

**Five points beyond the conditional theorem, and what is shown of each.**

1. The key file's and the request's honest provers and their simulators, and all three meeting the five conditions together: met inside the proof of the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe`, from which, with `refusedHandsOutNoProof`, `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest` follows, at one copy of the table [read]; no theorem states it by name, and the simulators are said to exist, not written (gap 75).
2. The link's result at the digest that `verify` itself reads: `AtPublicFiles.LinkLaid.linkLaidAtOnce` is at its one digest. The derivation of the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe`, and so of `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest`, is at no one digest: it holds at every value of the public reading (`plug_roadPremises`, AtFullSide/CompletenessAndZeroKnowledge/HonestProversAndSettingsOf.lean:108) [read]. These values include the digest that `verify` reads [read in part].
3. The provers' own errors: the simulators' errors are 0, and the stops 0, 0 and at most 2^-174 a reply (hashing's share of the error has its bound for any provers). Completeness of the same provers: gap 39.
4. The step from the whole public key (roots, OOD seeds and answers, the first table's proof, link proofs) to a system whose verifier is `verify` and whose key is the 256-bit commitment: met for the compiled system at the honest part, by name (`OnBytes.FrontRoad.privacy_frontId`, Johnson/FrontRoad/FrontId.lean:83; `slot_zeroKnowledgeOn_of_relationOn`, Johnson/FrontRoad/Slot.lean:179; `zeroKnowledgeOn_slotSystemF`, Johnson/CompletenessAndZeroKnowledge/OneHashByFirstBlockTestFront.lean:308; `compile_flashProve_eq`, Johnson/CompletenessAndZeroKnowledge/FinalRoadProof.lean:34) [read]. The Lean verifier's own system has a constant honest side (gap 55).
5. Open: batches, which are what ships: no statement here covers them, and none follows as a corollary (8). Coins derived from a seed are outside these theorems.

**Outside the scope:** that the C++ and CUDA programs do what a prover's side in Lean would say; that the hash may be treated as a random oracle that a simulator programs.

**Known attack or counterexample, for all twelve parts.** None known that shows more than the design itself declares visible (docs/what-is-not-hidden.md), with the protocol's conditions kept: each mask random, secret and used once; each table within its limit; secret seeds independent. Only (1) was attacked on purpose; elsewhere "none known" means none reported. Each of (7) to (12) is no attack on the protocol and no doubt of it: it is a statement that is missing, can be met trivially, or is typed for another object. Where a Lean statement, as typed, fails, or is vacuous or met trivially, that is a fault of the statement, not of the protocol.

What gives pause at (1), (3), (4) and (5) depends on a technical report on this protocol that is not public (gap 16), which nothing under lean/ can show.

### 15 (1) · The OOD answers

- **The limit; proved in part.** Beyond the head's results, all with fresh random masks, no theorem on hiding is shown to apply to the committed tables' OOD answers.
  - The library's theorems with an OOD sample are conditional (LIMITS.md, L14).
  - No Lean theorem covers the OOD answers as the programs make them; of the honest provers written in Lean: gap 39.
  - No theorem in this repository shows that the two premises of `rec_main_zk` can hold together where the OOD phase is not empty (Hiop/RecWhole.lean:61-62).
- **Proved nearest to it.**
  - At `verify`'s own configuration, at every OOD seed, with the pads (a row's pad is the mask on its OOD answer) inside the row as the format's pages lay them, and with no premise on the public files: the distribution of the OOD answers together with the encoded table at up to 44,040,191 positions, over uniform pads and encoding randomness, does not depend on the weights (`AtPublicFiles.Reads.hidE_filesAt`, E2E/ReadsAtFiles.lean:122; the clause `hides`, Hiop/RecConf.lean:168-175). That is one above what a copy opens at its query bound (10). At 44,040,192 it fails, for a single table, at one OOD seed and one pair of matrices (`AtPublicFiles.AtFiles.not_hidE_own`, E2E/ObjectGuardsSeeded.lean:98); that the whole result fails there, or that anything of the weights would show, is not proved. This is a statement on the encoding, before any protocol and any hash.
  - Before any hashing, at full size, with Lean's encoder and fresh pads, what is shown of the committed tables alone, within a budget of opened columns, has the same distribution whatever the weights, with fixed-message provers (`AtPublicFiles.AtFiles.tables_own_named`, E2E/TablesAtOwnObject.lean:203). That result has one OOD seed shared by all rows of a table, each row with a pad of its own, the copies, which encode the same weights, and the columns that link proofs open. It covers no prover's message, the link proofs' among them, alone or together with the tables. What goes with it:
    - It supposes, for each table, an OOD seed chosen from nothing hidden, then fresh uniform pads and encoding randomness, one OOD sample, and one set of public files for the table's random values. As run, seeds and opened positions are hashes over roots that depend on hidden values, and the random values are derived from the committer's secret seed by a pseudo-random function.
    - At the edge of the budget every random value of a table is spent [read], so none is left to cover what a real prover says of that table. The run's own random values must cover that. For the three honest provers written in Lean, Lean proves it, before hashing and for an honest verifier (gap 39).
    - A theorem says that the encoder and the protocols of that result are those of the input at which `verify` calls the compiled verifier. But no verifier reads the encoder, the pads or the map that makes the OOD answers. That they are the protocol's rests on a comparison with the format's pages [read]; no program's file was compared.
    - It is stated at the written copies of the two stored sets, and at all public files of the one proof format and model size that `verify` takes. Where `verify`'s own tests on these files fail, the key file's and the proof file's verifiers open no column; the OOD samples, the columns that link proofs open and those that the environment picks itself are covered all the same. That the tests pass is proved at those written copies and at the first written copy under every other policy in scope (gap 73); at all other files in scope it is not proved.
    - The Lean statement says any two matrices. Beyond the matrices that stand for weights this holds only because the provers are fixed-message provers: with real provers it must be cut to matrices in range [paper]. Two matrices that differ only outside the base field have the same tables.
  - After hashing, the head's result says that what the public key and the fixed-message provers' proofs show of the committed tables (roots, OOD answers, opened columns) can be simulated without the weights. It covers nothing that the three provers would compute from the weights in their rounds. That the view contains these roots, answers and columns is not proved [read]. That each produced proof opens such a column is proved for the scheme's own run and for one verifier that makes no hash query, not for every verifier. After hashing the hashed scheme in Lean too gets the OOD seeds by hashing the root; its random values stay fresh.
  - The conditional theorem's simulator (`OnBytes.WholeKey.simWholeKey`, Johnson/WholeKey/Wrap.lean:276) is constructed differently from the one argued below. By its files' opening comments it queries the OOD seeds as the committer does and sets no answer there. What it answers without consulting the oracle's table are the honest side's hash queries that contain a secret string.
  - The premises of `rec_hvzk_whole` are met together at `verify`'s own object, cut to the matrices in range, by the fixed-message provers alone.
  - At an OOD phase that is not empty no theorem of this library states that the hypotheses of `rec_main_zk` can be met together (Hiop/RecWholeMain.lean:22-23) [read], and the step that joins them to it is not proved in this repository.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known, with the protocol's conditions kept.
  - The step that is not proved, as it is argued [paper]: each sum's coefficients and the public factor on its mask are hashed from the root of the table's hash tree, under which the masks lie. By that argument the masks' independence of the OOD seed is no assumption of its own: it is what a simulator gets by programming the random oracle, as in the zero-knowledge form of the BCS compiler.

    The OOD seed is the hash function's answer at a query that contains the table's Merkle root. That root is fresh, because every leaf beneath it is salted: but for a small chance nobody has asked the hash function there before. So a simulator can sample the OOD seed itself, and with it the OOD answers and every value that a later proof will open; within the query bound their distribution does not depend on the weights, by the pads and the encoding randomness (for one table at `verify`'s configuration that is the first theorem above). It then takes a random word that has these values at these positions, hashes the whole tree over that word's salted leaves (the salts keep what is never opened from being guessed and tested), and sets the hash function's answer at that query to that OOD seed.
  - What gives pause: the argument for the simulated tree is on paper for a toy protocol alone, of a single row in a single table, which publishes no inner node of the tree; this repository does not contain that proof. The toy leaves out one OOD seed shared by all rows of a table, each row with a pad of its own (the mask on its OOD answer); the copies, which encode the same weights; and the link proofs between them. It is carried over to this protocol, and to the inner nodes in the key file as coming out of the same simulated tree, by reasoning [paper]. It is not written in Lean. On what the tree's published upper layers tell of the masks, the technical report (gap 16) has no word.

### 15 (2) · The honest side of the IOP that `verify` runs

- **The limit; proved in part.** This part goes with gaps 10 and 14. The protocol's three honest provers are written in Lean, as definitions and not as programs (`AtPublicFiles.AtFiles.HonestTerm.honestProvers`, E2E/TheHonestProver.lean, at `thePartsAt`, E2E/KeyArgumentsAt.lean). The honest committer is `compile`'s own, at the honest part `honestProversAndSettings` (AtFullSide/Face/HonestProversAndSettingsValue.lean); no theorem ties it to a program. Of the compiled system at that honest part zero knowledge is proved (gap 39). The Lean verifier's own system has a constant honest side (gap 55): index privacy can be stated of that system in Lean, but the statement would be about that constant side, and not about the committer and the provers of the six lines. Nor has Lean a prover's side of the protocol as the programs run it.
- **Proved nearest to it.** The link proof's prover: the head. No file of that prover says how likely any random value is: every pad, tail, mask and salt in them is a free variable or a written constant, and where they say that such values fit, they mean lengths, ranges and the format's own tests on bytes, which constants can meet too. No result on hiding rests on these files alone. `homeExactAt` draws those values uniformly and holds for all encoding randomness of both tables. The committer and the prover of the second form of the compiler: (8).
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known, with the protocol's conditions kept. What gives pause: that 219 random values in a kept row (a row of an oracle message in a request's own rounds that later segments read: the example of (10)) suffice is argued [paper]; no Lean statement names that number.

### 15 (3) · Derived masks

- **The limit.** Lean assumes fresh random values. The protocol as run derives its masks from the committer's secret seed by a pseudo-random function, so hiding can be computational at best there. A statement there takes a computational notion of hiding and a further assumption, neither of which this library has.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known, with the protocol's conditions kept, and the doubt is light. What gives pause is of wording: read literally, the definition of derived values in the technical report (gap 16) excludes the rule by which the protocol as run derives them; no fault of that rule is named [paper].

### 15 (4) · Clipped gap

- **The limit.** No result on hiding that this page names speaks of an output that a tolerant policy admits and that is not the model's own, so the clipped-gap policy is open.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known, with the protocol's conditions kept. What gives pause: by design each answer is one yes-or-no fact about differences of the model's hidden scores, which a client who chooses the checked tokens can narrow by repeated requests. The policy itself reveals that. What the simulator is given there is not defined [paper: the technical report (gap 16)]. (What the definition that this library follows answers: "The twelve, sorted".)

### 15 (5) · A rejected request, and a prover that stops a batch

- **The limit; proved in part.** A rejected request (in Lean's terms, a refused one) is covered, well-formed or not, for the Lean prover and one request to a proof: `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest` stands at every request; at a refused one the reply is the empty proof, and the simulator is told one bit for each request (served or refused), which depends on the weights (gap 39). Of an ill-formed request the corollary with the label shown (`zeroKnowledgeWithLabel`: served, failed or other tokens; gap 39) says nothing; the form with the label shown at every request is a theorem beside it (`finalZeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest_of_oneBit`, Johnson/Face/Checks.lean). No proved statement on hiding covers a prover that stops a batch. (For a prover that stops a batch there is no definition: the definition that this library follows has no batches. What it answers for the rest of this part: "The twelve, sorted".)
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known, with the protocol's conditions kept. What gives pause, concretely:
  - As run, the prover finds out while proving, and the batch's other proofs are then made in a run in which a failure has occurred. In the technical report (gap 16) it rejects by a public rule on the weights and the request, forms no proof and runs no verifier on such a request, and no moment is named [paper].
  - Any client can send such a request. For a prover that stops whole batches one term of the written bound on hiding comes out below 128 bits (a bound, not a leak), and for some stops no bound is stated.

### 15 (6) · A second commitment, and more proofs than the bound

- **The limit.** A second commitment to the same weights is open (LIMITS.md, L42). So are more proofs under one commitment than a theorem's bound allows: Lean's prover keeps no count (after compiling: 688,127 replies, on one copy of the table). All proofs under the commitment count: beyond the numbers of the head and of gap 39 no numeral is stated, not even for the proofs made before the bound was passed. No number is stated for more than 2^64 queries. The bound is on a statistical distance at a given number of queries to a random oracle; it is not a security level.
- **Known attack or counterexample.** None known, with the protocol's conditions kept, and the doubt is light. What gives pause: several commitments are covered on four conditions that no program checks [paper].

### 15 (7) · The goal itself can be met trivially as typed

- **The limit.** What the simulator is handed (`Description.shown`) and the error are free: in `PackageZeroKnowledge`, the one goal on zero knowledge alone (`PackageBundle` asks it with soundness and completeness), the simulator is handed `D.shown` of the weights, which no goal bounds, and the error has no range, so alone this form can hold for a trivial reason (Ladder/Package.lean:164-166, :183-185; Ladder.lean:370-373). A statement in the form `ZeroKnowledgeOn` (VerifiableInference/MoreStatements.lean:151-153), of any system, can be met in the same ways: its simulator is given the system's public parameters of the weights, which the form does not bound, and its error is a free parameter [read].

  In the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` (gap 39) both are fixed: the compiled system has no public parameters, so in the experiment the simulator is handed nothing of the weights (it may depend on the public files), and the error stays at or below 2^-154 within the line's limits; in its corollary `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest` the simulator is told one bit for each request. What the first paragraph tells is no attack on the protocol and no doubt of it: a statement that can be met trivially.
  - For example where `shown` returns the weights, or at the error 1, or with an honest side that sends nothing (the constant honest side of gap 55: `Shown := Unit`, the error 0) [paper].
  - Zero knowledge alone, without completeness of the same system, measures nothing (as Ladder/Package.lean:24-25 says of soundness): so the zero knowledge among the six top statements measures nothing by itself.
  - Ladder.lean has no goal on hiding (Ladder/Package.lean:182). One goal is typed for every IOP and proved of none, in the form of `ZeroKnowledge`: `PackageZeroKnowledge` (Ladder/Package.lean:183) [counted].
  - A key file's OOD sample has no place in the view: it is covered only as far as a proof carries it, and not at all before the first proof (Ladder/Package.lean:174-176). No statement in this repository gives what a committer publishes beside the commitment a place in the view.
  - A tighter form of the goal is not proved. It would hand the simulator nothing that depends on the weights beyond the three public files, of which the scale file and some lines of the parameter file are fitted to the weights, and would hold the error to a bar. Even so it would say nothing about what a committer publishes beside the proofs, and the constant honest side would meet those two clauses. Of the compiled system at the honest part the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` has both clauses (gap 39).
- **Proved nearest to it.** For three of the conditions on the honest prover in the conditional theorem of (8) the analogous statement is a theorem, for every IOP of the type: honest provers that send fixed messages and never stop meet them at error 0 (`OnBytes.WholeKey.silent_good`, `OnBytes.WholeKey.extEnc_silent`, Johnson/WholeKey/Foes.lean:383, :341).

### 15 (8) · The compiler has two forms, and no theorem leads from one to the other

- **The limit; proved in part.** No Lean theorem proves zero knowledge or index privacy at the two IOPs of Johnson/Flat/TwoSizes.lean, at which the theorem on `innerCompiledVerifier` (gap 13, 3) is taken. For the protocol's three provers together, at the IOP that `verify` runs: gap 39.
  - `verify` is built with the second form of the compiler (gap 40). What is proved on hiding of an argument system whose verifier is `verify` is the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` with its corollary `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest` (gap 39). For the theorems before compilation see (9) and (11).
  - The conditional theorem's key is the whole public key and its inputs are fixed before the oracles are sampled, while `verify`'s key is the 256-bit commitment and `verify` first reads parameters off the hash function. In the derivation of the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe`, and so of `zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest`, both differences are bridged, for the compiled system (the head, points 2 and 4; that the values of point 2 include the digest that `verify` reads is [read in part]) [read]. In that line and in its corollary too the weights and the public files are fixed before the hash function is drawn, and the verifier has no hash query before the commitment (gap 39).
  - For this protocol the 20 conditions are met by the protocol's own three provers inside the proof of the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` [read]; no theorem states it by name (gap 75).
  - A committer for the second form exists (`committerOverTypedOracles`, Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/CommitOverTypedOracles.lean:195). The prover beside it (`proveA`, Johnson/Prover/All/Defs.lean:139) takes its three strategies as arguments. The committer's randomness and its salts come from two enumerations that are arguments of which nothing is asked. Outside the compiled system at the honest part (gap 39) no statement on hiding names it, and outside that system the sampler behind its coins is a constant one wherever a closed system takes it in.
  - At `theWideDigits`, the system with `verify` as its verifier of which the two statements that speak of openings are proved (gap 55), the honest committer's sampler is the constant 0 (`placeHolderNumberCoins`, Johnson/Goal/Conditions/WideOnto.lean:110; `digitCoins`, Johnson/Goal/Conditions/WideDigits.lean:158): whoever guesses the weights can compute the commitment and compare. So these two statements do not speak of a committer that hides, and zero knowledge would fail there at every error below about one half, as soon as the verifier may make as many hash queries as the committer does (at most 2^48: `commitWithin_withTerms`, Johnson/Goal/Conditions/DescribeTerms.lean:214; `honestBudget`, Ladder.lean:247) [paper].
  - Limits of the conditional theorem itself (more: gap 75); they go with the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` and its corollary too, which are derived from it [read]:
    - One instance in each proof, a bounded number of proofs for each key. The programs prove instances in batches, and nothing is said of what a client learns about the other instances of a batch. With two instances in a proof the statement with this simulator would be false [read], so no corollary of it covers several instances. Past the number of copies times the sessions that a copy serves, the error is 1 or more [paper]. With two copies or more it is large before that number is reached, since the prover draws its copy at random (Johnson/WholeKey/Zeta.lean:12-13, :58) [paper]. At one copy the line's error stays at or below 2^-154 up to that number, within the line's limits (gap 39).
    - Its coins are uniform and independent. Its random oracles are typed: each class of hash queries has one of its own. The committer, the prover and the verifier are Lean terms, and no theorem ties them to a program. A committer that publishes its key only after a check of its own has passed, or that starts over after a stop, is another committer, and no part of the error accounts for it. The simulator's running time is not bounded. Nothing is said on what the public inputs themselves tell of the index.
    - A use of it names its range of indices, which must contain two indices or more and every index that has a true instance. Nothing is said of an index outside the range, and no part of the error accounts for that.
    - For two indices of the range with the same public parameters, under one environment valid at both, the real views are shown only to be within twice the error (`OnBytes.WholeKey.real_real`, Johnson/WholeKey/Half.lean:56). So an error below 1/2 is necessary, not enough. A prover that tells its index in all but a share of its proofs is ruled out where twice the error plus that share is below 1 (`OnBytes.WholeKey.FoeScheme.one_le_of_event`, Johnson/WholeKey/FoeScheme.lean:83) and no sooner [paper].
    - Its proof also rests on the hashed argument's general prover, in 26 further files under OnBytes/Prover/ and Johnson/Prover/: a Lean term that runs whatever honest provers it is handed. It is no prover of this protocol; what the theorem is about is decided there.
- **Proved nearest to it.** `OnBytes.WholeKey.wholeKeyPrivacy` is stated with the second form's verifier, behind a re-packing of key and proof, at general inputs. Its committer and prover are its own. By name, all 20 conditions are proved together for toy examples and, for the protocol as Lean describes it, in the two cases of the head: fixed-message provers, and fixed-message provers with the link proof's honest prover; not by name, but inside a proof, they are met by the protocol's own three provers (gap 75) [read].

### 15 (9) · At `verify`'s configuration three honest-side theorems as typed say nothing

- **The limit.** At `verify`'s configuration `rec_honest`, `rec_main_zk` and `rec_hvzk_whole`, as typed, say nothing: clauses that they ask fail there. This is no attack on the protocol: three statements typed for another object. A prover written in Lean does not make any of the three apply as typed. (What is proved of the three provers and their simulators: gap 39.)
  - `verify`'s configuration is of the type `Hiop.ConfigurationWithOutOfDomainSample`. `rec_honest` and `rec_main_zk` ask `ConfigurationWithOutOfDomainSample.Props`, with its clauses `held_hold` and `dcd_enc`; `rec_hvzk_whole` asks `WiredR`, with `dec_enc`. These three clauses fail there: `held_hold` by a result that is not proved in this repository, the others [paper]. That a full-size matrix with an entry 200 is out of range is proved (`not_inRange_200`, Johnson/Top/H/KeyMeaning.lean:161); that the type of indices there contains such a matrix is joined to that failure by no theorem. The opening comments of some files state the failure, among them Johnson/Top/H/KeyAny.lean (:27-30) on `held_hold`; OnBytes/Flat/CodingHonest.lean and Johnson/TypedOracleCompiler/RecCompile/CommitOverTypedOracles.lean on `dec_enc`.
  - Besides, `rec_honest` and `rec_main_zk` speak only of objects built from five typed sub-protocols, which `verify`'s IOP is not.
  - The proofs use less than the statements ask. The proof of `rec_hvzk_whole` reads the other three clauses of `WiredR` alone (Hiop/RecWholeProof.lean:442-473, :703-704); `rec_main_zk` uses `held_hold` and `dcd_enc` only to derive `dec_enc`, the clause that is not read (Hiop/RecMainProof.lean:57-62, :117). Of the configuration's own honest side (`realLawsR`, Hiop/RecWholeProof.lean:66) no statement that asks only the clauses that are read is in this repository.
  - A general theorem beneath asks no `WiredR` (`tv_viewR_le`, Hiop/RecWholeProof.lean:676-680: for any real side that is near the simulated runs, `LawsR.Near`, :396-407). Missing is, first, its application to that honest side from the three clauses that are read (`near_realR`, Hiop/RecWholeProof.lean:442-444, asks `WiredR` whole); second, that the three clauses and good simulators hold at that configuration for a real prover. That is shown of the fixed-message provers alone.
- **Proved nearest to it.**
  - `ReadsR` is a theorem at that configuration, for all provers, at all public files in scope (`AtPublicFiles.Reads.reads_filesAt_scope`, E2E/ReadsAtFiles.lean:222).
  - With the index type cut to the matrices in range, `dec_enc` is a theorem, and `rec_hvzk_whole` applies (`AtPublicFiles.AtFiles.tables_atFiles`, E2E/WholeViewAtFiles.lean:171).
  - A further theorem asks nothing of the index decoder (`Hiop.Seeded.whole_all_seeds`, Hiop/Seeded/WholeProof.lean:348): it asks `ReadsR`, provers that have the encoder's tables, and good simulators.
  - At this IOP the premises of the last two are shown of the fixed-message provers alone.

### 15 (10) · Hiding's own conditions on the parameters stand in no statement

- **The limit.**
  - The conditions (OnBytes/Params/Gate.lean; OnBytes/Plan/RangesGate.lean) include random entries against opened positions, mask entries against masked scalars, the leaf salt's length, and the bound on how often the honest approximate range proof fails. They need not stand in `verify`. They stand on its way all the same: where the gate (`protocolDataWellFormed`) accepts, the proof file's plan is read back from the value of one call of `configure` (`pseat_name`, Johnson/Goal/Description/ProofSeat.lean:25; `descPure_configure`, Johnson/Desc/ProofFile/Law/PlainMade.lean:117), whose text rejects public files that violate one (OnBytes/Params/Configure.lean:141-149). No theorem joins the three.
  - No lemma says that at every set of files in scope none of these conditions is missed. Without it a statement on hiding, at a small error, can be false at such files.
  - Nothing in Lean keeps count of a table's opened columns over its life; the programs keep that count, in a file for each table.
  - As run, the first of these conditions has no slack at any main or mask row of any proximity test, by construction: the encoding randomness is sized as the least that covers the opened positions (`tail_for`, blocks/numbers/codes.h, as blocks/frame/derive.h and blocks/frame/lin_frame.h call it; docs/format/, 16.1: at the example files every inequality of conditions 1 and 2 is an equality). So the missing lemma is no formality.
  - One example: 219 elements of encoding randomness for 219 opened positions (docs/format/, 8.5: group `link`, stage 1, r 219 and t 219, as the verifier's own planning call gives it at the default setting's full-size file). That row is a main row of an oracle message in a request's own rounds (its first Fiat–Shamir hash chain). Later segments of these rounds (a segment is one sub-proof's part) read the same row, each from a fresh encoding with new encoding randomness, by the programs' own comment (blocks/frame/hiding_conditions.h); so the count is of one encoding.
  - That row is not a row of a committed table, whose query bound is a separate count. At the full-size example a copy opens 44,040,190 columns at that bound, and its encoding randomness covers 44,040,191, with one column to spare (docs/format/, 16.1, condition 5). That is said of what a copy opens. The result on the committed tables of (1) bears 44,040,191 columns and not one more: that is said of the encoding randomness.
- **Proved nearest to it.** For the committed tables' count alone a part of that lemma is a theorem: at all public files in scope the verifiers read at most 62, 62, 62 and 64 columns, against 44,040,191 (`AtPublicFiles.Reads.reads_filesAt_scope`, E2E/ReadsAtFiles.lean:222).

### 15 (11) · Which verifier, and which IOP

- **The limit.** `rec_main_zk` is a statement before compilation, and gives none after it. No theorem leads from `rec_main_zk` to the conditions of `OnBytes.WholeKey.wholeKeyPrivacy`. A second sample on one table (gap 41) is outside `rec_main_zk`.
- **Proved nearest to it.**
  - `rec_main_zk` (Hiop/RecWholeMain.lean:33) is honest-verifier zero knowledge of the interactive protocol, with one OOD sample for each encoded table by the form of its experiment (Hiop/RecWhole.lean:24-25; `EnvR`, Hiop/RecWhole.lean:80-94).
  - `OnBytes.WholeKey.wholeKeyPrivacy` carries IOPs with an OOD sample through hashing: a conditional theorem with conditions of its own. They are of another kind than what `rec_main_zk` concludes: each simulator's output must have exactly the honest output's distribution, at every value of the encoding randomness, but for a set of verifier messages of bounded measure.

### 15 (12) · The salt at the start of a Fiat–Shamir hash chain

- **The limit.** Where a chain's run value is a hash over 32 random bytes (section 1.1: a request's first and third chains and the link's chain), that these bytes are fresh is a requirement on the honest prover. A statement on hiding must ask it of the prover's side. Lean's honest prover, a definition and not the program (2), has no such bytes: it draws each run value itself among its coins and sends it, and the zero-knowledge theorem is about that prover, with the hash function drawn at random; of the program `cp_prove` nothing is proved. No verifier needs to test that the bytes are fresh. For a hash chain outside that theorem, that one salt at the start suffices is argued, not proved: the source (ePrint 2025/536) proves it for a duplex sponge and states the analogue for a chain in a remark, without a proof (docs/limits-all-rows.tsv, row H51).

### The twelve parts in the terms of the definition

The definition that this library follows is named in CREDITS.md and typed in ZKCP/Defs.lean. There weight hiding is called index privacy. It is a property of an argument system (a committer, a prover and a verifier over a random oracle), not of an IOP: one simulator, handed the index's public parameters, the number of sessions and the instances, makes the index verification key, the oracle's answers and every proof.

- **`ZeroKnowledge`.** The environment sees as much as the definition's, and the key is narrower. Whoever picks the instances sees all that the adversarial verifier has seen, as in the definition. Beyond the definition that party may decline. It is a function with no coins of its own, where the definition's environment may toss coins (`zeroKnowledge_iff`, VerifiableInference/AgreementZeroKnowledge.lean:303): by its type it covers fewer environments, and no theorem of the library carries this form over to the others. Its key is fixed at 256 bits. It has no witness, so the privacy is that of the index alone. It is proved of no system.
- **`HasIndexPrivacy`.** `ZeroKnowledge` implies it (`indexPrivacy_of_zeroKnowledge`, VerifiableInference/AgreementZeroKnowledge.lean:384). It is weaker than the definition in spite of its name, and for the general forms the converse fails (a made-up scheme: ZKCP/Separation.lean).
- **`ZeroKnowledgeOn`.** It has `ZeroKnowledge`'s text for a smaller relation. No goal reads it [counted]; the line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe`, which is no goal in that sense ("Terms used on this page"), is written in it (gap 39). There its instances are the requests that can be served with the model's own output. That `HasStrongIndexPrivacyWith` for that relation gives it is a theorem (`zeroKnowledgeOn_of_privacy`, VerifiableInference/ZeroKnowledgeOn.lean:73). So whether a request can be served is membership in the index's language, which is told with the instance.

  The line `zeroKnowledgeOnCanServe` states it of the compiled system at the Lean honest part, for an error that stays at or below 2^-154 within the limits, at all public files in use, with no premise left (gap 39; LIMITS.md, L18). In another form, `ZeroKnowledgeBelowWhere`, with one bit for each request told to the simulator, zero knowledge is proved of the compiled system at the Lean honest part within 2^-154 (`zeroKnowledgeAtEveryRequest`, a corollary of that line and `refusedHandsOutNoProof`; gap 39). That form is not `ZeroKnowledgeOn`.
- **The index** is the weights.
- **Its public parameters** are `shown` together with the three public files, which are fixed before the weights and the simulator. The scale file and some lines of the parameter file are fitted to the weights, and what they tell lies outside every statement on hiding.
- **The index verification key,** as the goals type it, is the 256-bit commitment ID alone. The key file and the file of the one copy that a proof uses, each with its OOD sample, are sent inside that proof, as far as a proof's form carries them. That asks less than the definition, where the key is all that the committer publishes: before the first proof the view contains nothing but the ID, and a copy that no proof uses never enters it (7).
- **An OOD sample** is something that the simulator must make, not a public parameter.

### The twelve, sorted

This sorting closes nothing. Which parts the theorems of gap 39 meet: gap 15.

- **By layer:** all twelve are of the protocol as written in Lean; (1), (2), (3), (5), (10) and (12) also tell, four of them at "as run", where the programs differ from what Lean assumes.
- **Inside the definition** (which of them are open: gap 15): (1), (2), (7) to (12), and of (5) a prover that stops at a request that can be served: its stop is a value of the proof, which the simulator must make equally often.
- **Falls under the definition's computational form, which this library does not define** (ZKCP/Defs.lean:12-13): (3).
- **Needs another relation, not another definition:** (4). What (4) finds not defined has an answer in the definition that this library follows: the simulator is handed the request and nothing more. What membership then tells of the hidden scores counts, in that definition, as told by the instances; no statement bounds how much that is.
- **Beyond the definition,** which has one run of the committer and no instance outside the language: of (5) a rejected request and a stop of a whole batch (the definition has no batches), and of (6) a second commitment. The count in (6) is the definition's number of sessions, and lies inside it: the error is a function of that number, and beyond its bound the theorem says nothing. The definition's prover keeps no state, as Lean's keeps none, while the programs' keeps a counter.
